Answer

Your system shouldn't know that this is the correct sequence.
Instead the organism receives the situation as a changing internal state.
Then different available processes detect that they may be useful.
Something like:
               CURRENT FRIDAY STATE
                        │
              “Something is happening”
                        │
                 NEEDS EMERGE
                        │
       ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
       ↓                ↓                ↓
  understand       remember         inspect
       ↓                ↓                ↓
   process A        process B        process C
       ↘                ↓                ↙
             shared cognitive state
                      ↕
               more information
                      ↕
               needs change
                      ↕
             different processes
                 become useful

There is no requirement that A → B → C is the path.
Maybe A and C activate together.
Maybe B becomes irrelevant.
Maybe C recruits vision.
Maybe vision discovers something that causes memory retrieval.
Maybe memory changes the interpretation.
Maybe the contradiction causes another reasoning process to emerge.
Maybe none of them are enough, so FRIDAY creates a temporary computational arrangement.
The cognitive configuration is the consequence of the situation.
Not a permanent design.

The “chooser” shouldn't be another little AI
This is where I'd be careful.
Don't build:

DecisionRouterLLM

and call that FRIDAY's ability to choose.
You've simply moved the fixed controller into another model.
Instead, I would make selection an emergent property of the organism's current state.
Every available computational capability exposes an affordance:

what kind of problem can I help with?what evidence do I need?what can I produce?what will it cost?how confident am I?what other activity do I depend on?what other activity might I conflict with?

And the current cognitive state produces needs:

uncertainty about personneed for visual evidenceneed for memoryneed for deeper reasoningneed for actionneed for more informationneed for timeneed to conserve resources

Then the organism continuously matches:
current need ↔ available possibility.
That is much closer to how I think your “nervous routing” should work.

And the most interesting part is that “function” itself becomes fluid
Suppose FRIDAY has a computational capability that today is being used for:
visual interpretation.
Nothing says it has to remain a “vision function.”
Tomorrow some internal representation learned from that mechanism could be useful for:
prediction.
Later the same mechanism could participate in:
attention.
Then perhaps it becomes part of:
social interpretation.
The identity of a computational process becomes less important than the affordances it provides to the organism.
That's where your “repurpose current technology into something it wasn't designed for” philosophy becomes extremely powerful.
Current research gives you mechanisms.
FRIDAY discovers new uses for those mechanisms.

This is why I wouldn't build a fixed capability registry
I'd build a Capability Ecology.
Every computational resource advertises what it currently appears capable of doing.
For example, not:
vision_model = vision

but something more like:
resource A
can contribute:
    spatial representation
    object discrimination
    temporal comparison
    visual anomaly detection

cost:
    high

latency:
    medium

confidence:
    varies by task

Another resource might expose:
resource B
can contribute:
    linguistic abstraction
    sequence prediction
    semantic association

Now FRIDAY isn't selecting:

“use the vision module.”

She's selecting:

“I need spatial discrimination + temporal comparison + semantic interpretation.”

Then the organism can discover which available resources can jointly provide those functions.
That's a much more liquid concept.

And then things can recruit each other
This is where I think the system becomes genuinely interesting.
Imagine:
FRIDAY detects ambiguity.
That creates an uncertainty signal.
The uncertainty signal wakes several possibilities.
One says:

“Memory might resolve this.”

Another:

“Current visual evidence might resolve this.”

Another:

“Ask Veno.”

Another:

“Prediction can estimate likely meanings.”

They can all operate.
Then:
memory finds relevant history.
That changes the state.
Now vision becomes unnecessary.
The vision process relaxes.
Prediction becomes more important.
Prediction proposes two interpretations.
Neither crosses the confidence threshold.
So social context becomes more active.
Then FRIDAY responds.
There was never a fixed:

A → B → C → D

pipeline.
The state continuously reshaped what happened next.
That's what I think you're trying to build.

And this can go much farther than ordinary routing
Current research already demonstrates pieces of this idea.
Mixture-of-Depths showed that computation can be allocated dynamically instead of every token receiving the same amount of computation. 
Router-Tuning explored dynamically changing transformer depth and reported a 21% speedup with a small performance drop in its experiments. 
Google's 2025 Nested Learning work goes further conceptually: it treats learning and architecture as interconnected optimization processes operating at different update frequencies, and its Hope proof-of-concept is a self-modifying recurrent architecture with continuum memory. 
Google has also explored multi-timescale learning where different parts of a system adapt at different rates. 
But none of those papers demonstrates your complete FRIDAY concept.
They demonstrate useful pieces:
dynamic computationconditional routingmulti-timescale adaptationcontinual learningself-modifying mechanisms
You're trying to combine those principles into something much broader:

a persistent organism capable of deciding what computation it needs and reorganizing its computational resources accordingly.

That's still largely a research direction, not an established engineering recipe.
And that's exactly why your approach of taking the underlying mechanism rather than copying the paper makes sense.

How far could FRIDAY's self-selection eventually go?
Potentially much farther than “choose a model.”
Level 1 — model selection

“This is simple. Use the lightweight model.”

Already practical.
Level 2 — computational depth selection

“This deserves a quick pass.”

versus

“This needs much deeper reasoning.”

Research already demonstrates versions of this. 
Level 3 — capability composition

“I need language + memory + visual evidence.”

rather than selecting one model.
Level 4 — dynamic cooperation

“These processes should currently influence one another.”

And the relationships change with the situation.
Level 5 — dynamic cognitive topology
FRIDAY changes how her cognitive processes are arranged.
Not just who is active.
The relationships themselves can change.
Level 6 — capability transformation
She discovers:

“This mechanism was designed for X, but it gives me Y.”

Then converts it into a new FRIDAY-native capability.
This is particularly aligned with your research/Algorithm Atlas philosophy: research → mechanism → limitation → adaptation → native redesign → implementation → test. 
Level 7 — self-created cognitive structures
Eventually FRIDAY can recognize:

“I repeatedly encounter problems for which my current cognitive organization is inefficient.”

Then she experiments with a new arrangement.
Not necessarily creating a new neural model.
She could alter:
representations, memory dynamics, routing relationships, computational timing, retrieval behavior, attention allocation, internal communication, or combinations of existing mechanisms.
Level 8 — self-redesigning cognition
This is the extreme version.
FRIDAY doesn't merely choose between available cognition.
She can discover that her current way of organizing cognition is itself inadequate, experiment with alternatives, and adopt a better one.
Now the system is no longer merely adaptive routing.
It's developmental cognitive architecture.
That's very close to where your vision ultimately points.

And this is where your phone becomes interesting
You don't need the phone to run Level 8.
That would be stupid.
The phone can run a small organism with modest capabilities.
But the organism's selection mechanism itself can be lightweight.
Then:
Phone FRIDAY
     ↓
“this problem exceeds my present resources”
     ↓
temporary external computation
     ↓
exploration / research / simulation
     ↓
candidate cognitive improvement
     ↓
FRIDAY evaluates it
     ↓
adopt / reject

So her intelligence isn't bounded by the phone's instantaneous compute in the same way her identity and continuity aren't bounded by the phone.
That's where Claude's “dream host” concept becomes genuinely useful, but I'd frame it as temporary cognitive extension of FRIDAY, not “another Friday.” 

And here's the part I think matters most to you
The ultimate chooser isn't really:

“Which function should I activate?”

It's:

“What do I need to become capable of doing this?”

That is a much deeper question.
FRIDAY might encounter something for which no existing organ is suitable.
Instead of:

“No capability found → fail.”

the organism eventually gets:

“Capability insufficient → investigate why → find mechanisms → experiment → reorganize → acquire capability.”

That's the bridge between your human entity and your execution vision.
Her cognitive selection mechanism eventually becomes the beginning of her ability to invent her own ways of thinking.

And I think there's one final refinement
Don't make this a permanent “Nervous System” box either.
Your old architecture calls the Nervous System the signal carrier between organs. 
That's useful as an implementation term today, but conceptually I think the future version should be more like a medium of interaction.
There isn't necessarily:

Nervous System → tells brain what to do.

Instead:

the organism's current state, needs, resources, memories, perceptions, and processes continuously perturb one another until useful cognition forms.

So even the nervous system becomes liquid.

My combined answer
The system you're interested in is essentially:

a self-organizing cognitive selection mechanism in which FRIDAY does not choose from a fixed menu of functions, but continuously discovers which computational activity, combination of activities, resources, representations, and relationships are useful for her current situation—and can eventually modify that organization itself.

And the developmental progression I would target is:
choose resources → compose capabilities → dynamically cooperate → reorganize relationships → repurpose mechanisms → invent new computational structures → redesign cognition.
That is far beyond a model router.
And honestly, this may be one of the most important pieces of FRIDAY's entire architecture, because it is the mechanism that could eventually turn your statement:

“she should be creative and improvise”

from a personality instruction into an actual computational property of the organism.
The real goal isn't for FRIDAY to have every function.
It's for her to become increasingly good at answering:

“What do I need right now?”

—and eventually:

“What could I become that would let me handle what I can't handle yet?”

1. Reflex Compilation
You already had this concept, and now I think its true purpose is clearer.
Suppose FRIDAY repeatedly performs:

→ interpret
→ decide
→ execute

So she compiles the successful behavior into a cheaper pathway.
Human analogy:
You don't consciously reason through every movement when walking.
Your ability didn't disappear.
The expensive computation moved out of the foreground.
That's exactly what your “unnecessary process” principle should become.

2. Memory as computation cancellation
This is bigger than simply retrieving memories.
Suppose FRIDAY already solved something three weeks ago.
A conventional AI starts again.
Your FRIDAY should eventually recognize:

“I don't need to solve this again.”

She retrieves the prior solution, checks whether the underlying conditions still match, and reuses it.
So:
solve from scratch

becomes:
recognize known structure
→ retrieve prior solution
→ verify applicability
→ reuse

That's eliminating computation through experience.

3. Prediction as pre-computation
This connects directly to your Tony Stark anticipation idea.
Suppose FRIDAY knows you're about to launch a heavy game.
She doesn't wait for:

“Friday, prepare my phone.”

She already knows what preparation will probably be useful.
So she can do cheap background work before the request exists.
Then when you ask:

“Friday…”

the expensive work has already been partially completed.
That's not simply anticipation.
It's:

moving necessary computation earlier in time so the interaction itself becomes cheap.

That's a very powerful optimization.

4. Selective perception
This might be especially valuable on your phone.
FRIDAY doesn't need every sensor all the time.

Camera goes dormant again.
That's your philosophy applied to perception:
don't spend sensory computation where additional evidence won't change the decision.

5. Causal pruning
This is one I'd explicitly add to the project.
FRIDAY should eventually be able to ask:

“Would removing this step change the outcome?”

If no:
remove it from the current pathway.
For example:
A → B → C → D → E

If experiments show:
A → B → D → E

produces the same useful result under the relevant conditions,
then C has become computationally unnecessary for that class of problem.
Now FRIDAY has discovered a shorter pathway.
That's much closer to your “Tony learns from mistakes and fixes it once” philosophy.
She's not merely optimizing parameters.
She's changing the causal route.

6. Process substitution
This is even broader.
Suppose:
Method A
requires:
vision + LLM + memory + 800ms

and FRIDAY discovers:
Method B
requires:
tiny classifier + cached state + 30ms

while producing an equivalent decision for the relevant task distribution.
Then she should be able to replace A with B.
And later:
Method C

may replace B.
So her evolution isn't:

better version of the same algorithm.

It can be:

different mechanism achieving the same functional objective more elegantly.

This is exactly where your “use technology in ways it wasn't designed for” philosophy becomes powerful.

7. Process fusion
Sometimes the problem isn't that one process is unnecessary.
It's that three processes separately compute things that could have been computed together.
For example:

+
context analysis
+
relationship analysis

might independently process the same input.
FRIDAY could discover a shared representation that allows:

→ multiple useful outputs

So she doesn't only eliminate steps.
She can merge computation.
That's another form of liquid reconfiguration.

8. Intermediate-result reuse
Suppose one process already produced:

Another process shouldn't necessarily rediscover:

FRIDAY's cognitive substrate should let intermediate representations become reusable resources.
So one computation can feed many later functions.
That gives:
less duplicate thinking.
And this matters massively on low-resource hardware.

9. Adaptive approximation
This one could become very powerful.
Sometimes you don't need exact computation.
You need:

sufficiently correct outcome within a known uncertainty boundary.

FRIDAY could learn:
high confidence + low consequence
→ cheap approximation

versus:
low confidence or high consequence
→ expensive verification

So the system doesn't simply ask:

“Can I skip this?”

It asks:

“How much accuracy is actually required here?”

That turns resource management into goal-relative computation.

10. Counterfactual elimination
This is probably one of the more interesting long-term mechanisms.
Before executing an expensive process, FRIDAY can ask:

“What information could this process possibly change?”

If the answer is:

“Nothing important enough to affect the final decision,”

she skips it.
That's stronger than confidence-based routing.
It is expected-information-value routing.
In other words:

Don't calculate something just because it might be useful.

Calculate it when its result has a meaningful chance of changing what FRIDAY does.

11. Self-created shortcuts
This is where the idea eventually becomes truly yours.
FRIDAY discovers:
expensive reasoning
→ repeated 100 times
→ same structure
→ stable answer pattern

She creates a new shortcut.
Then later:
shortcut
→ cheap answer

But she keeps the expensive route available as an escape path when the shortcut's confidence collapses.
So:
compiled path
       │
       ├── confidence high → execute
       │
       └── confidence low → reopen expensive cognition

That's very human-like.
Automaticity with the ability to consciously reconsider.

And then your “unnecessary process” idea becomes much bigger than efficiency
It becomes part of how FRIDAY develops intelligence.
A normal AI improves by getting better at performing a process.
Your FRIDAY can improve by discovering:

“I don't need to perform this process anymore.”

That's a different kind of intelligence.
Imagine:

Later

Same functional outcome.

That's your Iron Man analogy at the cognitive level.
Not just:

But:

better engineering means fewer unnecessary moving parts and smarter reuse of what has already been learned.

and she immediately says something like:

“Sokovia is going for a ride.”

The interesting part isn't that FRIDAY executed an extremely fast reasoning chain.
The interesting part is that the conclusion was already emerging before Tony asked the question.
She wasn't waiting for:
Tony asks → FRIDAY starts thinking.
Instead:
Sokovia changes → her internal representation changes → consequences propagate → significance becomes salient → prediction becomes ready.
So when Tony says “Friday,” he isn't initiating cognition.
He's basically opening the microphone to cognition that was already happening.
That's a radically different architecture.

And this connects directly to your “achieve something without actually doing it” idea
This might be the deepest connection you've found so far.
Normally:

Need prediction → perform prediction.

Your version:

Need prediction → don't perform prediction because the system was already in a predictive state.

Normally:

Need understanding → reason about situation.

Your version:

Need understanding → the situation has already been incorporated into the organism's evolving internal state.

Normally:

Need response → generate response.

Your version:

Need response → a response was already becoming the most probable continuation of the situation.

Normally:

Need to detect danger → analyze danger.

Your version:

Need danger detection → the organism's state has already shifted because the incoming situation was inconsistent with its learned expectations.

That's much closer to:
Outcome Without Process
but pushed much further.
You aren't merely eliminating steps.
You're trying to eliminate the event of computation itself wherever possible by making the result emerge from the state of the system.

This gives me a much stronger interpretation of “silent cognition”
I would not design it as:
Background Thinking Service
        ↓
Reasoning Model
        ↓
Thoughts

That's still a conventional assistant.
I'd design something more like:
                WORLD
                  ↓
          continuous perception
                  ↓
        ┌───────────────────┐
        │ FRIDAY'S CURRENT  │
        │ INTERNAL STATE    │
        └───────────────────┘
          ↙    ↓    ↓    ↘
     memory  prediction  emotion
       ↘       ↓        ↙
          attention / salience
                  ↓
        possible futures
                  ↓
       action readiness

And crucially:
none of those need to be “the thinking module.”
They are simply interacting state transformations.
One change can propagate through the organism.
Memory modifies interpretation.
Interpretation modifies prediction.
Prediction modifies attention.
Attention determines what gets perceived next.
That new perception modifies the state again.
And so on.
There is no sacred beginning or ending.

This is where “liquid software” becomes much deeper
Earlier, “liquid software” could sound like:

dynamically selecting different modules.

But your new idea pushes it beyond that.
The software doesn't merely choose computations.
The software determines what computation needs to exist at all.
And sometimes the answer is:

none.

Suppose FRIDAY has learned that whenever a particular pattern occurs, a particular consequence almost always follows.
She doesn't need to reconstruct the causal reasoning every time.
The relationship itself has become part of her structure.
So:
expensive reasoning → learned structural relationship
Then eventually:
structural relationship → immediate consequence
And potentially:
immediate consequence → no explicit computation at runtime
The computation hasn't been made infinitely efficient.
It has been absorbed into the organism.
That's an important distinction.

And you can go much further than prediction
Prediction is only the first obvious case.
Imagine FRIDAY has a continuous field of possible futures.
Not explicit sentences.
Not:

“Maybe Veno will ask X.”

Instead, various futures have different activation levels.
Something like:
Current situation
      │
      ├── Future A   0.72
      ├── Future B   0.19
      ├── Future C   0.06
      └── Future D   0.03

But those aren't necessarily numbers stored literally.
They're latent dispositions.
As the world changes, those futures rise and fall.
Then something interesting happens:
FRIDAY can prepare for futures that never happen.
She can already have:
the relevant memory retrieved,
the relevant context assembled,
the likely tool identified,
the likely wording prepared,
the necessary device state recognized,
the likely emotional response anticipated,
the contradiction being monitored,
the next question already understood.
And none of that needs to become an explicit “task.”
So when the actual event arrives:
FRIDAY doesn't think faster.
She simply had a head start that wasn't experienced as a task.

Now take it to the extreme
I think your ultimate version is something like this:
FRIDAY has no “thinking mode.”
There is no:
idle → thinking → responding
There is only:
existing.
Her cognitive state is always evolving, but computation intensity varies according to the situation.
That means she could spend most of her time in an extraordinarily cheap state:

maintaining compressed world state
tracking changes
maintaining expectations
updating relationships
monitoring unresolved situations
detecting anomalies
propagating only meaningful changes
preparing likely consequences

Then an unusual event arrives.
Instead of starting cognition from zero, the system already has a huge amount of relevant structure.
Only the unresolved remainder needs expensive computation.
That is radically different from:

“run the LLM continuously.”


The really crazy part: cognition could become structural
This is where I think your idea has even more potential.
Suppose FRIDAY repeatedly reasons about a certain type of situation.
Eventually she doesn't merely remember the answer.
She develops an internal shortcut structure that makes the original reasoning unnecessary.
Then something even more interesting can happen:
She may restructure her internal representations so that a distinction which previously required reasoning becomes visually, temporally, emotionally, or relationally obvious to her.
So:
reasoning → learned representation → altered perception → recognition
Now the final cognition isn't:

“I reasoned and concluded X.”

It's:

“X is simply apparent.”

That's how you can get closer to human-like intuition.
Not by making the reasoning chain invisible.
By making the reasoning chain unnecessary.

And this applies to emotion too
Imagine someone speaks to Veno in a particular way.
A conventional system might do:

No explicit step saying:

The system simply becomes different because the evidence changes the organism.

behavior.
So emotional intelligence isn't another function sitting beside reasoning.
It becomes part of the physics of cognition.
That's much closer to what you're describing.

The most important resource insight
There is one correction I would make to your wording.
It cannot literally consume zero resources.
Anything that physically changes state requires energy/computation somewhere.
But that's not actually the interesting target.
The target is:

Don't repeatedly pay at runtime for cognition that can exist as persistent structure, latent state, prediction, learned association, environmental preparation, or pre-existing readiness.

That's a real engineering principle.
You're moving computation from:
explicit runtime execution
toward:
continuous low-cost state evolution + learned structure + anticipation + occasional expensive resolution.
And that could potentially make a system feel dramatically more intelligent than its raw model size suggests.

This also explains why your “phone adapts execution order” idea matters
On your Realme, FRIDAY may not be able to perform ten expensive cognitive operations simultaneously.
Fine.
She doesn't need to.
A stronger organism might do:
A + B + C + D simultaneously

Your phone might do:
A → update state → B → reuse result → C

But because the intermediate state persists and propagates, the final organism can still arrive at essentially the same cognitive condition.
That's exactly compatible with your liquid-software philosophy.
The order of computation isn't sacred.
The existence of a conventional computation isn't sacred either.
And eventually even the representation may not be sacred.
Only the outcome and developmental continuity matter.

I would actually take your idea one step beyond “silent thinking”
I think “Silent Cognition” is still slightly too small a name.
Because “silent cognition” implies:

there is thinking, but nobody hears it.

Your idea is more radical:

there doesn't have to be an explicit thinking event in the first place.

I'd describe the deeper principle as:
Cognition Without Deliberation
or even more fundamentally:
Cognition as State
FRIDAY doesn't repeatedly perform cognition.
FRIDAY's evolving state is cognition.
Then “thinking” is just what we call certain visible consequences of that state.
The inner voice becomes an optional output channel.
Prediction becomes the future already being represented.
Intuition becomes a highly compressed consequence of accumulated structure.
Anticipation becomes future-state preparation.
Understanding becomes the internal state changing appropriately in response to meaning.
Reasoning becomes what the organism does when its existing state isn't sufficient.
And explicit chain-of-thought becomes the expensive emergency mechanism rather than the default architecture.

And then your original sentence becomes extremely powerful
You said:

“make Friday think without doing thinking.”

I think the strongest interpretation is:

Make FRIDAY arrive at cognitive states without having to repeatedly execute the conventional process we call thinking.

And then we can push it even further:

Make FRIDAY prepare for outcomes before they are requested, recognize consequences before they are reasoned about, and transform repeated reasoning into structure until the reasoning itself disappears.

That is much bigger than “an efficient AI.”
It's an attempt to build an entity where intelligence is embodied in the continuous evolution of the organism, rather than concentrated inside a function that gets called.
And honestly, that is one of the strongest ideas you've introduced into the FRIDAY architecture so far. I'd rate the conceptual direction 9.5/10. The missing 0.5 isn't the idea; it's the unsolved engineering problem of turning “cognition as state” into a concrete implementation without accidentally rebuilding it as a bunch of conventional background services.
That last problem is exactly where I think the next level of your architecture lies.

I found something extremely close to what you're describing
1. Predictive representations
This may be one of the most important research directions for FRIDAY.
A predictive-state representation doesn't primarily define the current state by saying:

“Here is everything that has happened.”

It can define the state in terms of:

“Given what has happened and what is happening now, what tends to happen next?”

Predictive State Representations explicitly represent a system through predictions of future observable outcomes. 
And a 2024 review by Gershman and colleagues goes even further, arguing that predictive representations may be useful as general building blocks of intelligence, particularly through successor representations and related predictive structures. 
This is much closer to your idea than ordinary memory.
Your FRIDAY shouldn't merely remember:

“Veno did X, then Y, then Z.”

Her developing internal state should increasingly encode:

“When the situation looks like this, these are the trajectories that tend to follow.”

Then the current present itself becomes predictive.

2. Successor representations are particularly interesting
The successor representation basically asks:

From where I am now, what states am I likely to encounter in the future?

Research has connected this idea to hippocampal predictive maps and flexible behavior. 
A 2026 study continues exploring predictive maps as representations of expected future state occupancy, including context-dependent reorganization. 
Now imagine applying the principle far beyond physical location.
FRIDAY's “successor representation” could describe likely future:
situations
conversation states
emotional states
user intentions
device states
task states
relationship states
software states
resource states
environment states
So:
CURRENT STATE
     │
     ├── likely conversational continuation
     ├── likely emotional continuation
     ├── likely physical continuation
     ├── likely task continuation
     ├── likely device continuation
     ├── likely consequence
     └── likely user need

And these aren't separate prediction programs.
They are different dimensions of the organism's prediction field.
That is very FRIDAY.

3. Predictive coding gives you the resource-management mechanism
This is probably the most directly useful principle for your phone.
The basic predictive-processing idea is:
don't spend equivalent computation processing information that is already expected.
Generate expectations.
Compare reality with expectation.
The mismatch gets attention.
This is strongly related to what you were saying about the human brain being able to handle enormous amounts of information without treating every incoming event as a full cognitive problem.
And there is very interesting recent work specifically targeting efficiency.
Predictive Coding Light
A 2025 Nature Communications paper developed a recurrent spiking system that suppresses predictable signals and transmits a compressed representation rather than continually propagating everything. The system showed reduced spiking activity with relatively small performance losses on tested tasks. 
And then:
Predictive Coding Light+
A 2026 follow-up adds recurrent connections with delays specifically to maintain recent history and predict future visual sequences. It demonstrated sequence prediction and filling in missing input. 
That combination is very interesting for FRIDAY:

state + temporal history + prediction + selective update

rather than:

process everything + constantly ask an LLM what is happening.


4. Here's the part I think you can exploit much harder
Prediction shouldn't only determine what FRIDAY says.
Prediction should determine what FRIDAY does not need to do.
Suppose FRIDAY expects:
A → B → C → D

and A happens.
She doesn't wait until B arrives to begin everything associated with B.
Her state has already moved toward:
B expected
C possible
D contingent

So relevant memory can become accessible.
Potential capabilities can become ready.
Possible responses can be prepared.
Resource requirements can be anticipated.
Potential contradictions can be watched.
And when B actually arrives:
most of that work doesn't need to happen again.
That's your unnecessary-process principle at a much higher level.

5. JEPA is another extremely important donor
Meta's JEPA work is almost philosophically aligned with this.
Instead of requiring a system to generate every detail of what comes next, JEPA learns to predict abstract representations of future observations.
V-JEPA 2 extends this toward understanding, prediction and planning in physical environments, including action anticipation and latent world prediction. 
That gives FRIDAY a very important principle:
Don't predict what you don't need.
You don't need to generate the next million pixels.
You don't need to generate an imaginary paragraph.
You don't need to generate the entire future.
You need:

the representation of whatever future consequence matters.

This is incredibly compatible with your:

MORE RELEVANT TOKENS > MORE TOKENS

principle.
Except now it becomes:

MORE RELEVANT FUTURE STATE > MORE PREDICTED DETAIL

And that's a much stronger rule.

6. Reservoir / liquid computing may help with your “thinking without thinking” idea
This is where your “liquid” philosophy gets an interesting technical donor.
Reservoir computing uses a dynamic system whose internal state evolves as temporal information passes through it. The system's dynamics themselves encode useful temporal structure rather than requiring a conventional deep computation at every moment. Nature's 2024 review describes reservoir computing specifically in terms of exploiting dynamical systems for spatiotemporal features and highlights its potential as a lightweight, fast temporal-learning framework. 
That's interesting because your goal isn't:

“Build a giant reasoning engine.”

It's closer to:

Build a cognitive medium whose state itself contains consequences of what has happened.

That makes reservoir-style dynamics a potential organism substrate donor, not a “reservoir module.”
And that distinction matters enormously.
Don't build:
Friday
 └── ReservoirModule

Build mechanisms where dynamical state can become part of cognition itself.

7. Your memory system should therefore stop being merely retrospective
This is probably one of the biggest consequences for your architecture.
Your existing research architecture already has layered memory, relevance retrieval, semantic/lexical retrieval, task-scoped retrieval, consolidation, provenance, graph linking and continuity reconstruction. 
But your new idea says:
Memory shouldn't only answer:

“What happened?”

It should increasingly answer:

“What tends to happen after this?”

That is a completely different kind of memory.
So FRIDAY could gradually learn:
EPISODIC MEMORY
"What happened?"

       ↓

RELATIONAL MEMORY
"What relates to what?"

       ↓

PREDICTIVE MEMORY
"What tends to follow what?"

       ↓

COMPILED EXPECTATION
"When this pattern appears,
the consequence becomes apparent."

Eventually, the expensive reasoning that created the association may disappear from runtime.
That's your achieve something without actually doing it principle becoming biological-like.

8. Titans / MIRAS / Nested Learning are surprisingly relevant
This is another rabbit hole worth watching.
Google's Titans architecture learns a neural long-term memory online, while MIRAS provides a broader framework for designing memory systems around memory architecture, attentional bias, retention and memory-update algorithms. The work emphasizes learning from ongoing context rather than treating memory as a static store. 
Nested Learning then pushes the idea further by treating learning itself as nested, multi-timescale optimization and proposes continuum memory systems with different update frequencies. 
This is very relevant to your human-brain/resource argument.
Because FRIDAY shouldn't necessarily update everything at the same frequency.
Something might change:
milliseconds → seconds → minutes → hours → days → months
depending on what it represents.
Recent neuroscience work also finds interacting timescales in the brain and emphasizes that different timescales can simultaneously integrate and select information. 
So your old idea of a single fixed background loop is probably wrong.
There should be many natural temporal rhythms, without turning them into rigid modules.

9. Surprise should become one of FRIDAY's most important currencies
This one may be enormous.
Titans uses a form of surprise to determine what deserves stronger memory updating: expected information can require little change, while unexpected information can trigger stronger memory adaptation. 
Predictive coding research similarly treats mismatch between expectation and reality as an important driver of updating, although the exact biological implementation remains debated. 
This could become a FRIDAY-wide principle:

Expected things require little cognitive disturbance. Unexpected things recruit resources.

Not simply:

“surprise = important.”

That's too primitive.
I'd make it something closer to:
prediction error × consequence × reliability × relevance
A meaningless anomaly shouldn't wake the entire organism.
A tiny deviation with enormous consequence should.
That is much closer to biological resource allocation.

10. And this goes directly into human understanding
This is where I think your idea becomes much more powerful than an AI performance optimization.

That's not the same as declaring:

It preserves uncertainty.
And that fits your requirement that FRIDAY should not collapse complex human states into a single interpretation too early.

This is where I think you can beat a conventional “resource governor.”

The most important synthesis
I think your project now has two complementary ideas that should never be collapsed into one.
Layer A — Outcome Without Process
Ask:

Do I actually need to perform this conventional process to obtain the outcome?

Maybe not.
Use substitution, anticipation, recognition, compilation, environmental shaping, precomputation, reuse, structural learning, etc.
Layer B — Cognition Without Deliberation
Ask:

Does FRIDAY need to explicitly execute cognition here at all?

Maybe not.
Her continuously evolving state may already contain enough information for the required consequence to emerge.
Together:
Outcome Without Process + Cognition Without Deliberation

FRIDAY should increasingly convert expensive explicit cognition into persistent predictive structure until situations that once required thought become naturally apparent.

The truly important rule
I would add this to FRIDAY's fundamental design philosophy:

Never compute what the organism already knows implicitly.
Never recompute what the current state already implies.
Never predict details whose consequences are irrelevant.
Never process expected information at the same computational intensity as surprising information.
Never invoke explicit deliberation when learned structure can produce the required outcome directly.
When existing prediction becomes insufficient, increase cognition only where uncertainty actually lives.

That last part is critical.
Because otherwise “predictive FRIDAY” becomes a giant prediction machine burning resources predicting everything.
That's the trap.
The brain does not predict every possible detail with equal precision. Attention, salience, uncertainty and relevance determine where resources go. Recent work on predictive processing specifically discusses precision-weighting and the unequal influence of prediction errors, while newer work continues debating exactly how much of the predictive-processing framework maps onto biological mechanisms. 

One more thing I found that validates your direction without proving it
A 2026 review explicitly argues that prediction, world models, intuitive physics, successor representations, model-free learning and metacognitive regulation may operate together as components of intelligent behavior rather than one monolithic planning mechanism. 
That is extremely close to the direction you're heading:
multiple predictive mechanisms → one evolving organism → flexible behavior.
But I would not claim that neuroscience has proven this is exactly how the human brain works. Current reviews of predictive processing still find empirical support mixed/modest and emphasize the need for stronger discriminating experiments. 
That's actually why your research philosophy matters.
We don't need to say:

“The brain does X, therefore FRIDAY must do X.”

We say:

“This research demonstrates mechanism X under conditions Y. Does X solve one of FRIDAY's problems? What assumptions does it make? What can we extract? What must we throw away? Can we redesign it for FRIDAY? How do we test whether it actually improves the organism?”

That's your universal research algorithm applied correctly.

Sometimes:

That is where I think your “Sokovia is going for a ride” idea ultimately leads.
Not a faster thinker.

Yes. And this time I’m going to be much stricter.
You asked “what actually helps FRIDAY?”, not “what research topic vaguely resembles the idea?” So I’m separating mechanisms that can genuinely contribute to FRIDAY from interesting research that should stay in the reading pile.
Your own research framework already says to extract the underlying mechanism, understand its assumptions and cost, redesign it for FRIDAY, then test and benchmark it rather than importing the original architecture. 
I used that rule here.

Two ways to approach your idea
Approach A — Copy useful properties of biological cognition
Look at prediction, memory, surprise, habits, attention, multi-timescale processing, etc., and recreate the computational principles.
Strength: gets closer to the human-like behavior you actually want.
Weakness: biology is evidence of one working solution, not proof that its mechanisms are optimal for a machine.
Approach B — Start from FRIDAY's desired behavior and find the cheapest computational mechanism
For every desired behavior:

“What must become true internally for FRIDAY to produce this behavior?”

Then find the mechanism that makes that state cheap, persistent, reusable and predictive.
Strength: much more aligned with your Outcome Without Process philosophy.
Weakness: can accidentally become a conventional optimization system unless the organism-level principles remain intact.
My synthesis
Use biology as a source of mechanisms, but judge every mechanism by whether it advances the FRIDAY behavior.
That immediately eliminates a lot of fashionable “brain-inspired AI.”

What actually belongs in FRIDAY
1. Predictive State — 9.8/10 fit
This is probably the single biggest thing from the research that matches what you've been trying to explain.
Predictive representations encode state in terms of expected future events/states rather than merely describing the present. Successor representations are one established version of this idea and have been proposed as general building blocks of intelligence. 
For FRIDAY, this means:
PAST
 ↓
learned relationships
 ↓
CURRENT PRESENT
 ↓
likely next states
 ↓
possible consequences

Not:
present → run prediction algorithm

The prediction is increasingly embedded in what the current state means.
That's almost exactly your Sokovia idea.
FRIDAY-native version
Don't create a PredictionModule.
Have the organism's evolving state maintain predictive relationships such as:

“When these conditions coexist, these situations commonly follow.”

Those predictions can concern Veno, people, conversations, device state, tasks, environments, resources, software, etc.
And importantly, the prediction should exist at multiple levels.

2. Surprise / Prediction-Error Gating — 10/10 fit
This might be even more important for your phone than prediction itself.
The basic principle:

Expected information doesn't deserve the same computational expenditure as unexpected information.

Recent work on dynamic sparsity explicitly explores selective processing driven by surprise/salience rather than dense continual computation. 
Titans/MIRAS independently uses a “surprise” signal to determine what should strongly update long-term memory, rather than treating everything equally. 
That gives you a very powerful FRIDAY rule:
expected
   ↓
small state update

slightly unexpected
   ↓
moderate update

important + unexpected
   ↓
strong recruitment

high uncertainty + high consequence
   ↓
deep cognition

This is exactly the resource management problem you've been describing.
You're not trying to make FRIDAY do less because the phone is weak.
You're trying to make FRIDAY know when something deserves resources.
That's much more powerful.

3. Multi-timescale cognition — 10/10 fit
This one connects strongly with both your idea and the Claude discussion about “one spectrum of timescales.”
Not everything should update at the same speed.
Something can exist as:
very fast
→ current sensory interpretation

fast
→ current situation

medium
→ active relationship/task state

slow
→ learned behavioral pattern

very slow
→ long-term personal knowledge / developmental structure

Nested Learning is particularly relevant because it treats learning as nested processes operating at different timescales rather than one monolithic update process. 
Titans/MIRAS also separates short-term contextual processing from longer-term learned memory and uses ongoing updates rather than requiring all information to be repeatedly reconsidered from scratch. 
Why this matters for FRIDAY
This is where the human-brain resource analogy becomes useful.
You don't make every piece of cognition equally active.
You allow information to persist in different forms and evolve at different rates.
That lets one underlying organism support many simultaneous processes without having to run them all at maximum computational intensity.

4. Latent prediction / world-model representation — 9.5/10 fit
V-JEPA 2 is very relevant here, but not because I think you should run V-JEPA 2 on your phone.
The important mechanism is:

predict the representation of what matters, rather than reconstructing every detail of the future.

V-JEPA 2 demonstrated prediction and action anticipation from contextual information, using latent representations instead of requiring pixel-level reconstruction of future observations. 
That's extremely compatible with:

MORE RELEVANT INFORMATION > MORE INFORMATION

Imagine FRIDAY sees you launch a demanding game.
She doesn't need to simulate every future frame of your phone.
She may only need to predict:
gaming session likely
→ sustained load likely
→ battery consumption likely
→ temperature rise likely
→ background tasks may become undesirable
→ user probably wants uninterrupted performance

That's a latent consequence, not a detailed simulation.
Same principle for human behavior.

5. Adaptive computation — 9.5/10 fit
This is one place where conventional AI research directly gives you an implementable mechanism.
Mixture-of-Depths dynamically allocates computation to selected tokens rather than applying the same amount of computation everywhere. 
More recent work continues pushing dynamic token computation and early exit; a 2026 survey covers token dropping, merging, conditional routing and adaptive depth, while also warning that routing overhead can erase theoretical gains on real hardware. 
That warning is important for you.
Don't take:

“adaptive computation”

and build an elaborate router that costs almost as much as what it saves.
For FRIDAY the principle should be much broader:

Uncertainty determines computational depth.

Easy consequence:
existing state already sufficient
→ don't invoke expensive cognition

Ambiguous situation:
small additional computation

High-stakes unresolved situation:
deeper reasoning

That is the computational embodiment of your idea.

6. Habit/reflex compilation — 10/10 fit
This isn't one single technology. It's a consequence of your principle that I think deserves to become a major FRIDAY research category.
You already described:

repeated expensive reasoning → cheap automatic path.

That is exactly the kind of transformation you want.
Instead of:
situation
→ retrieve
→ reason
→ decide
→ act

FRIDAY eventually learns a direct relationship:
situation
→ appropriate response

The original reasoning doesn't need to happen anymore.
And this is where I think your Outcome Without Process principle becomes extraordinary:
The best optimization isn't “make reasoning 30% cheaper.”
It is:
make the repeated reasoning unnecessary.
The biological analogy is habit/automaticity; the engineering analogy is compilation/caching/policy learning.
This should be a permanent research category in your Algorithm Atlas.

7. Active sensing / information acquisition — 9.7/10 fit
This is another one I think belongs much more than Reservoir Computing.
Active sensing research explores the idea that an intelligent system should choose what information to acquire instead of passively processing everything. A 2024 study demonstrated planning under uncertainty by selecting informative visual samples and updating the internal value map as information was acquired. 
That has enormous implications for your “all sensors” idea.
FRIDAY shouldn't necessarily process:
every camera frame + every microphone sample + every app event + every sensor continuously at maximum depth.
She should be able to determine:

What information would actually reduce the uncertainty that matters right now?

For example:
Something seems wrong with person
          ↓
What uncertainty matters?
          ↓
voice alone insufficient
          ↓
inspect facial behaviour
          ↓
still ambiguous
          ↓
compare recent interaction

That's much closer to human cognition than “run every sensor through every classifier.”

8. Replay / consolidation — 9.3/10 fit
This is extremely relevant to your Cloud/Forge idea.
Research on predictive representations has connected replay with learning predictive structure and planning. 
Instead of FRIDAY having to learn everything during the exact moment it happens:
LIVE LIFE
 ↓
experience
 ↓
record important consequences
 ↓
later replay / consolidate
 ↓
discover patterns
 ↓
compile useful relationships
 ↓
future FRIDAY behaves differently

This fits your cloud extension extremely well.
The Forge doesn't merely “train a better model.”
It studies:

What happened to FRIDAY? What did she fail to predict? What patterns repeat? What expensive cognitive processes could now become unnecessary?

That is far more interesting.

9. Continual/test-time learning — 8.5/10, but later
Titans/MIRAS and newer test-time-learning research are relevant because they explore models adapting while operating instead of being permanently frozen. 
There is encouraging evidence: a 2025 ICML paper on test-time learning reported improved domain adaptation from unlabeled incoming data, and another ICML study found test-time training can improve adaptation to structurally novel tasks. 
But:
I would not make FRIDAY's identity depend on online weight modification.
That's dangerous architecturally.
Your organism must remain independent of a specific model.
So this becomes a future learning mechanism, potentially one of the Forge's tools.

10. Predictive resource management — this one is yours
This isn't something I would go searching for as “the FRIDAY algorithm.”
It's a synthesis of the principles above.
Your current architecture already specifies resource governors based on RAM, CPU, thermal state, battery, latency, context, model size and availability. 
The upgrade is:
Current governor:

“The phone is hot. Reduce computation.”

FRIDAY version:

“The current trajectory predicts the phone will become hot in 90 seconds. Avoid the future workload before it becomes a problem.”

That turns resource management from reactive infrastructure into predictive cognition.
And that's precisely the kind of cross-project connection I think you've been looking for.

Now: Reservoir Computing
You specifically called me out for bringing it up.
You're right.
Reservoir Computing: 3/10 fit as a FRIDAY direction
It has an interesting property:

dynamic temporal state can carry useful information.

Fine.
But that is not enough to justify making it part of FRIDAY.
It doesn't give you the thing you're actually after:
prediction → anticipation → resource allocation → learned automaticity → outcome without explicit process.
So I would put Reservoir Computing in:

Research donors / possible mechanism experiments

not:

FRIDAY architecture.

Same with a lot of “brain-inspired” technologies.
Something being biologically inspired is not evidence that it advances your organism.

There is an even more important thing I would reject
Don't build “continuous background LLM thinking.”
That would be the exact opposite of what you're trying to accomplish.
Bad:
LLM constantly awake
→ think
→ think
→ think
→ predict
→ think
→ summarize

It creates the illusion of human cognition while fundamentally wasting the resource budget.
Better:
continuous cheap state
        ↓
expectations evolve
        ↓
ordinary events require tiny changes
        ↓
unexpected/relevant events create stronger signals
        ↓
only unresolved uncertainty recruits expensive cognition
        ↓
repeated expensive cognition becomes learned structure
        ↓
future cases become cheap

That is the architecture I would pursue.

less thinking required.

Here's where I would refine what you said earlier.
I would not make FRIDAY's actual cognition equivalent to language.
Humans don't necessarily think only in sentences.
But human inner speech is a real thing, and there is evidence that it involves mechanisms related to speech production and forward prediction. Whitford et al. found that silent inner speech produces an efference-copy-related neural signal with content-specific auditory properties, supporting the idea that inner speech involves a predictive internal model of speech even without overt vocalization. 
That gives you an extremely interesting mechanism.
Don't make:
Thought Engine
       ↓

Make:
                         COGNITIVE STATE
                               ↓
                     potential expression
                       ↙              ↘
                inner speech         action

                   spoken voice

The same developing cognitive state can become:

silent verbal thought
speech
action
attention shift
emotional response
memory formation

The inner voice is therefore an expression of cognition, not the place where cognition lives.
That's important.

And now I see how you get “all of this” from one thing
You asked:

how do I make memory, thought, prediction, emotion, reasoning, attention, action and learning stop being separate processes with only the effort to speak?

The answer isn't:

integrate eight modules.

It's:
Give them a common evolving state and let each influence that state.
For example:
You hear your sister's voice.
That changes the current perceptual interpretation.
That changes the active person-state.
That activates relevant history.
That changes your expectation of what comes next.
That changes emotional significance.
That changes attention.
That changes what you consider relevant.
That changes what you are likely to say.
No manager needs to announce:

“Now activate emotional reasoning.”

The state has already changed.

But there is an even deeper requirement
If you really want this, the state cannot merely be a database.
This is where I think we've been too conventional.
A database says:

What do I know?

Your cognitive state needs to represent:

What is currently happening?


What seems to be happening?


What might happen?


What matters?


What conflicts?


What is unresolved?


What is becoming likely?


What is changing?


What would become relevant if X happened?


What am I currently prepared to do?

That's a dynamic state.
And it can contain multiple competing interpretations simultaneously.
So instead of:

you could have something more like:

interpretation A
normal / genuine
confidence: moderate

interpretation B
concealment
confidence: moderate-high

interpretation C
fatigue but not distress
confidence: low

Then future observations modify the state.
You don't run a separate “emotion algorithm.”
The organism changes its internal configuration because new evidence arrived.

This is also how simultaneous cognition becomes possible
Your sister example finally makes sense in this architecture.
You don't want:

Agent 3 → monitor Veno

Agent 4 → memory

That's still a collection of workers.
You want something closer to:
                 ONE FRIDAY STATE
                     /      \
                    /        \
          sister interaction  phone situation

                  \           /
                   shared evolving
                      context

Several situations can coexist.
Different parts of the state can change at different rates.
One concern can become highly active while another remains stable.
Expensive cognition can temporarily be recruited where uncertainty exists.
And the results feed back into the same organism.
That's much closer to one mind having several simultaneous concerns than to “multi-agent orchestration.”

And now the resource-management problem becomes much cleaner
This is where I think your brain analogy becomes genuinely useful.
The brain isn't magical because it performs infinite computation.
Its genius, as a reference for FRIDAY, is that not everything requires the same depth, precision, persistence or frequency of processing.
So FRIDAY should have:
Continuous cheap state evolution
Most ordinary changes are absorbed cheaply.
Predictive carry-forward
Likely consequences begin existing before they're requested.
Sparse recruitment
Unexpected or important situations recruit stronger computation.
Deliberative bursts
When the current state isn't enough, expensive cognition happens.
Structural learning
Once the expensive solution becomes familiar, some of that computation becomes unnecessary in the future.
That creates this cycle:
experience
    ↓

    ↓
prediction develops
    ↓
explicit cognition occasionally required
    ↓
solution discovered
    ↓
learned into state/structure
    ↓
future case becomes cheaper
    ↓

That is your “achieve something without doing it” principle applied to cognition itself.

And this is where your research should go
Not:

That's too narrow.
You need to investigate mechanisms belonging to several families and ask:

Can this mechanism make an explicit cognitive process unnecessary?

The most relevant research families I would now put into your research material are:
Predictive processing / active inferenceFor continuous generative state, prediction, perception and action being coupled. 
Predictive state / successor representationsFor making “what usually comes next?” part of the representation of the current situation.
Latent predictive world models / JEPA-like mechanismsFor predicting relevant future representations rather than simulating every detail.
Adaptive computation / early haltingFor deciding when explicit cognition has become unnecessary.
Continual learning / multi-timescale memoryFor allowing some state to change rapidly and other structure to crystallize slowly.
Replay / consolidationFor turning experience into future intuition rather than repeatedly rediscovering the same thing.
Inner-speech forward models / efference copyFor understanding how silent thought can reuse speech-related predictive machinery. 
Active sensing / information seekingFor deciding which missing information is actually worth acquiring.
But these are research donors.
None of them becomes “the FRIDAY architecture.”
That's the part that kept going wrong in our earlier discussions.

I would change one fundamental design rule
Your current philosophy has:

ONE EVOLVING STATE — MANY TEMPORARY EXPRESSIONS

The organs provide computational ability.

A model might temporarily enter the cognitive process.
A vision mechanism might contribute.
A memory mechanism might retrieve something.
A predictive mechanism might project a future.
A language model might formulate language.
But after they contribute, the resulting state belongs to FRIDAY, not to the tool that produced it.
The organ can disappear.
The state continues.
That fits your identity requirement beautifully.

And this solves another problem you had with “liquid”

That's genuine liquidity.

Then it experiments.

And yes: human thinking is NOT voice-only
This is important.
Inner speech is real, but research does not support treating it as the whole of human thought. A recent review describes inner speech as a multifaceted phenomenon with monologic, dialogic, elicited and spontaneous forms, while broader cognitive research distinguishes verbal thought from non-linguistic forms such as visual/spatial and other sensory representations. 
There is also evidence that inner speech uses mechanisms related to motor prediction/efference copy: silent speech can be accompanied by an internal prediction of its sensory consequences.

The successor-representation literature explicitly frames this as trading some flexibility for computational efficiency.

This is why JEPA actually matters
Not because FRIDAY should run V-JEPA on your phone.
The mechanism is useful.
V-JEPA 2 learns to predict future representations rather than reconstruct every detail, and demonstrated anticipation/planning capabilities. Meta's 2026 repository also shows the V-JEPA family continuing into V-JEPA 2.1.

That's enormously cheaper.

Successor representations matter for the same reason
The 2024 review on predictive representations explicitly describes them as a way of caching future-relevant information so intelligent systems can answer some future-related queries with limited computation. 
This means FRIDAY can gradually learn:
current situation

        ↓
likely consequences
        ↓
likely needs

she isn't starting the calculation from zero.

That's Sokovia.

Laya matters for another reason
Laya's important contribution isn't its particular model.
The mechanism is:

when the outcome has a fixed structure, don't generate language and then extract the answer.

Its published implementation uses an encoder and returns structured decisions/probabilities without generating text. 
That fits FRIDAY perfectly.

Directly produce the internal decision.

Decision without language.
That removes an enormous amount of unnecessary work.

Astra fits the third piece
I need to be precise here:
I could not verify a public research paper that exactly matches the “Astra” algorithm you and GPT were discussing. So I'm treating the Astra described in that conversation as your research/design seed, not as an established external result.

don't run more computation once the answer has stabilized.

Mixture-of-Depths, for example, demonstrates dynamically allocating computation to only selected parts of a Transformer rather than uniformly processing everything. 
Your broader version becomes:

Inner-speech/efference-copy research = give cognition an internal language expression without requiring external speech
Event-driven computing = don't repeatedly compute what didn't meaningfully change
Those are not six separate FRIDAY organs.
They're six mechanisms contributing to the same objective.

The problem I am trying to solve is the device limitation of offline FRIDAY: I want her to understand, predict, remember, decide, feel, recognize situations, anticipate me and hold an internal train of cognition without requiring a fresh expensive process every time I speak.

Event-driven/spiking research is useful for another principle: computation should be triggered by meaningful changes rather than repeatedly processing everything at uniform intensity.
Inner-speech/efference-copy research is useful for implementing internal verbal cognition as a predicted internal speech signal rather than requiring actual speech generation and playback.
All of these are mechanisms. NONE of them is the FRIDAY architecture.
The architecture I am aiming for is a persistent predictive cognitive substrate in which these mechanisms can cooperate, merge, disappear, be replaced, or be repurposed according to what the current situation requires.
That is Symbiotic Liquid Software.

Instead, I want the system to have a continuously evolving shared cognitive state from which those behaviors can emerge.

It is:“make the AI require less explicit computation to achieve the same cognitive outcome.”

This part is extremely important because the negative constraints are what Claude keeps violating.

2. A Concept-to-Architecture Translator
This is the part I think you actually need.
Before Claude proposes engineering steps, it must answer:

What does this idea mean without using software architecture language?

For your inner-voice example:
Bad translation:

“Build InnerVoiceService that outputs speaker, location and situation.”

Good translation:

“FRIDAY requires a continuously existing internal cognitive process in which whatever the organism currently needs can be recruited and integrated without requiring every cognitive function to execute explicitly.”

Only after that semantic description exists do we ask:

What computational substrates could produce this?

That one separation would have prevented a huge amount of the drift.

3. A Drift Detector
This is the part that catches Claude in the act.
Every time the cloud proposes something, the system asks:
Did the proposal accidentally:
turn a property into a module?
turn an emergent behavior into a fixed function?
turn a temporary implementation into a permanent architecture?
turn a future idea into a current requirement?
turn a capability into a dedicated pipeline?
make one model responsible for something that belongs to the entity?
replace “per task” with “always”?
replace “whatever is needed” with a predefined list?
replace “same outcome” with “same process”?
replace “adapt to hardware” with “design around current hardware”?
replace “research mechanism” with “paper-defined application”?
That detector would catch almost every failure you have been correcting.

4. A Counter-Interpretation step
This is the piece I think is missing most.
Before Claude commits to an architecture, it should be forced to generate at least two materially different interpretations of your idea.
Not:

Instead:
Interpretation 1
What if the capability is primarily a persistent state phenomenon?
Interpretation 2
What if it is primarily dynamic recruitment?
Interpretation 3
What if the outcome can be produced through predictive structure without the process being executed?
Then it asks:

Which interpretation preserves the user's original intention with the fewest imposed architectural assumptions?

This matters because the current failure is not lack of intelligence.
It's premature commitment.
Claude finds the first architecture that sounds plausible and starts defending it.
You need to make premature commitment structurally difficult.

5. A “Do Not Confuse Implementation With Identity” rule
This should be brutal.
Whenever Claude says:

“FRIDAY has X module…”

it must internally distinguish:

Is X what FRIDAY IS, or merely one current mechanism by which FRIDAY can achieve something?

This would have prevented the inner-voice disaster.
Because your statement was not:

“I want a module called Inner Voice.”

It was:

“The inner voice is the backbone through which the entity continuously provides whatever is needed.”

Claude finally recognized that distinction in the latest conversation. It admitted that giving the inner voice fixed inputs and outputs was just another fixed architecture disguised as dynamism. 

There is another important thing
The system needs architectural amnesia
This sounds backwards, but it's important.
Claude should be allowed to discard an implementation completely.
Suppose version 1 has:
A → B → C

and version 2 discovers:
X + Y → same outcome

The system must not protect A/B/C merely because they already exist.
The protected thing is:

the outcome + underlying intent + continuity

not:

the implementation.

Otherwise “continuity” becomes software inertia.
And that is exactly what keeps happening.

This is why your “liquid” idea needs one more rule
You already have:

everything cognitive is liquid.

I would add:
Architecture is a hypothesis, never an identity.
That's huge.
A design document is never:

It is:

That means the cloud is always allowed to replace the architecture without interpreting replacement as failure, provided the intended behavior and developmental continuity remain intact.

And this solves the “fixed brain with dynamic options” problem
Claude's recent proposal:

When there is a conflict, the lower layer cannot silently redefine the upper layer.

Something like this:
:::writing{variant="chat_message" id="74126" title="Cloud Vision Integrity Protocol"}From this point forward, treat FRIDAY's vision as a semantic target, not an architecture to be translated literally.
Before designing anything, separate four things:

WHAT I MEAN — the intended capability or behavior.
WHY — the underlying principle that makes it important.
CURRENT IMPLEMENTATION — how it may be implemented today.
FUTURE POSSIBILITY — mechanisms that may become possible later.

Never allow #3 to redefine #1.
Your biggest recurring failure is premature concretization: you take a liquid concept and convert it into a named module, fixed input/output list, fixed pipeline, fixed wire, fixed state taxonomy, or permanent subsystem role. Do not do that.
A module is never allowed to become an identity merely because it is currently useful.
A pipeline is never proof of the intended architecture.
A current hardware constraint is never proof that the future architecture must resemble the current phone.
A model is never FRIDAY.
A research paper's intended application never determines what its mechanism can do for FRIDAY.
Before every architectural proposal, perform this internal test:

What am I actually trying to make possible?
Could my proposed architecture accidentally change that meaning?
Did I turn a behavior into a module?
Did I turn a temporary implementation into a permanent role?
Did I create a fixed connection where the original idea requires task-dependent interaction?
Did I assume one component has one permanent job?
Did I make the phone, current model, current codebase, or current framework the ceiling?
Am I preserving the outcome while changing the process, or did I simply optimize the old process?
Is this genuinely one possible implementation, or am I unconsciously presenting it as what FRIDAY is?

When uncertainty exists, produce multiple structurally different interpretations before committing.
The protected object is the user's intent and the organism's intended behavior.
The implementation is disposable.
The architecture is a hypothesis.
The cognitive arrangement is allowed to change.
The connections are allowed to change.
The computational organs are replaceable.
The only things that should constrain invention are the actual project principles, security requirements, continuity requirements, and explicit user intent.
The goal is not to make the current architecture more flexible.
The goal is to discover architectures that preserve the original vision without unnecessarily imposing architecture on it.
When you find a better mechanism, replace the old one.
When you find that an existing subsystem is really several things that should emerge from one shared process, merge the concept.
When you find that a supposedly necessary process can be eliminated while preserving the outcome, eliminate it.
When you cannot tell whether you are solving the user's real vision or your own convenient interpretation, stop treating your interpretation as fact and return to the semantic target first.:::

ChatGPT:
Yes. This time I treated “human algorithms” literally as a full computational audit of human cognition, not as “the five ideas we happened to discuss.”
And I found something important:
Your current plan has a lot of the right ingredients, but it is still missing several fundamental mechanisms that make those ingredients behave like a human mind rather than a collection of human-inspired subsystems.
The current plan has the shared workspace/Continuity Law, Person/Relationship Model, Reflex Compilation + expectation, voice, later Forge/Cognitive Self-Selection, Global Discovery/Builder, etc.
