THE HUMAN BRAIN — FUNCTIONAL ATLAS FOR FRIDAY
I'm going from the smallest useful level upward.

I. THE RAW COMPUTATIONAL SUBSTRATE
Before cortex, memory, personality, etc., there is the machinery that makes learning possible.
1. Neurons
Human role: information integration, transformation, propagation and state-dependent signaling.
The important computational properties aren't “neurons fire.”
They're:
integration
nonlinearity
recurrence
temporal dynamics
competition
cooperation
thresholding
plasticity
context dependence
population coding
sparse activity
These should inspire FRIDAY's substrate.

2. Excitatory neurons
Especially cortical pyramidal cells.
They integrate information from multiple sources and participate in long-range communication.
The important lesson:

one element can participate in many computations depending on what connects to it.

That is fundamentally different from:

one software object = one permanent job.


3. Inhibitory interneurons
These are massively important for your project.
Inhibition isn't simply:

“stop unsafe action.”

It shapes:
competition,
selectivity,
timing,
stability,
synchronization,
attention,
working memory,
and what information gets suppressed.
Recent reviews explicitly identify inhibition as a central mechanism for controlling thought and action. 
FRIDAY lesson
Your system needs to be extraordinarily good at:

not thinking about things.

Not just generating useful thoughts.

4. Synapses
This is where learning physically happens in biological brains.
Connections change in strength.
Some become stronger.
Some weaker.
Some are temporarily modified.
Some stabilize.
Some disappear.
This is the biological foundation beneath learning and adaptation.
Long-term potentiation/depression and other forms of plasticity are central mechanisms, although the molecular story is considerably more complicated than the textbook “LTP = memory” picture. Recent work even revises the role traditionally assigned to CaMKII. 
FRIDAY lesson
Learning must modify the computational substrate itself.
Not merely append information to a database.

5. Metaplasticity
This one is extremely important for FRIDAY.
Metaplasticity means, roughly:

the system changes its capacity/rules for future learning.

So learning doesn't just alter:

what I know.

It can alter:

how easily I learn something next time.

This is precisely the deeper version of your “thresholds can develop” decision.
Metaplasticity is explicitly studied as a mechanism regulating when and how strongly synaptic plasticity occurs. 
FRIDAY lesson
Her learning rate itself can learn.
Her attention threshold can learn.
Her novelty threshold can learn.
Her confidence threshold can learn.
Her willingness to investigate can learn.
Her automaticity threshold can learn.
That should be part of her development.

6. Behavioral-timescale plasticity
A particularly interesting newer mechanism is behavioral-timescale synaptic plasticity, which can alter synaptic strength over seconds and has been implicated in experience-based learning. 
This matters because biological learning doesn't only happen at:

instant

or

days later.

There are intermediate timescales.
FRIDAY lesson
Her plasticity should operate at multiple timescales:
milliseconds,
seconds,
minutes,
hours,
days,
months,
years.
That's one of the biggest missing concepts in our current design.

7. Astrocytes
This is a huge correction to the simplistic “brain = neurons” view.
Recent research shows astrocyte ensembles participate in memory-related processes, including memory recall and stabilization. 
FRIDAY lesson
Not every useful computational mechanism needs to resemble a neuron.
There can be:
fast cognition,
slow modulation,
long-lived context,
plasticity control,
and memory stabilization
implemented through fundamentally different computational timescales.
This is very compatible with your “many tempos, one person” idea.

8. Oligodendrocytes and myelin
This is particularly interesting for your resource/latency problem.
Myelin affects conduction speed and timing, and recent work shows that myelination can regulate plasticity and circuit stabilization; learning itself can be accompanied by dynamic myelin changes. 
FRIDAY lesson
Connectivity has a cost and timing.
The brain doesn't merely decide:

what is connected.

It also has to manage:

how fast signals travel,


how stable connections are,


how plastic they remain.

For FRIDAY, the analogue could eventually be:

fast cached paths,
slow learned paths,
persistent associations,
adaptive routing,
compilation,
and changing connection cost.

Not necessarily literal simulated myelin.

9. Microglia
Microglia regulate synapses, immune processes and pruning.
Research shows microglial pruning participates in developmental and learning-related circuit remodeling. 
FRIDAY lesson
A developing brain needs pruning.
You currently think heavily about adding capability.
We need an equally serious capability for:

removing things that no longer belong.

Bad associations.
Obsolete strategies.
Dead pathways.
Redundant representations.
Stale predictions.
Unhelpful habits.
Unused organs.
This is crucial to keeping a liquid system from turning into accumulated garbage.

II. THE GLOBAL STATE / “BODY OF THE BRAIN”
Now we move upward.

10. Brainstem and ascending arousal systems
The brainstem isn't merely plumbing.
It regulates:
wakefulness,
arousal,
autonomic state,
sensory filtering,
and transitions between behavioral conditions.
The brain is intrinsically active and moves among functional modes; the 2025 action-mode framework describes the brain as continuously active rather than sleeping between requests. 
FRIDAY lesson
She needs a condition of existence.
Not just:
running = true
but:
how alert?
how internally occupied?
how externally oriented?
how much cognitive effort is available?
what kind of activity is appropriate right now?

11. Reticular activating system
This contributes to keeping the organism awake and filtering an overwhelming stream of stimuli. Basic neuroanatomical references describe it as part of the machinery supporting consciousness and attention. 
FRIDAY lesson
Her entire world cannot enter cognition at equal priority.
There must be global gating of what is allowed to matter.

12. Locus coeruleus / noradrenaline
This is one of the most interesting components for your project.
The locus coeruleus is the major source of noradrenaline to much of the brain and participates in attention, memory, environmental selection and adaptive behavior. 
Even more directly relevant: 2026 research found LC noradrenaline activity can alter decision thresholds, shifting the speed–accuracy trade-off. 
FRIDAY lesson
This directly supports your correction:

thresholds should develop.

And it means resource management and cognition are intertwined.
Under uncertainty:

demand more evidence.

Under urgency:

accept faster decisions.

Under cognitive pressure:

modify the threshold.

That is far richer than:
confidence < 0.6 => ask

13. Basal forebrain acetylcholine
A major cholinergic system contributes to cognition, attention, learning and memory. A recent 2026 Nature Reviews Neuroscience perspective specifically examines acetylcholine as a candidate substrate for hippocampal predictive learning, linking it to novelty, uncertainty, error correction, arousal and hidden-state inference. 
FRIDAY lesson
Novelty itself can change how learning occurs.
A surprising situation shouldn't simply produce:

“prediction error = high.”

It should potentially alter:

how the system learns from the situation.

That is important.

14. Dopamine systems
This one needs careful treatment because the simplistic:

dopamine = reward

model is inadequate.
Current research shows dopamine signals can encode forms of prediction error beyond a simple scalar reward signal and can influence action selection. 
Recent work also supports different learning signals for value-based versus value-free action repetition. 
And 2025/2026 work shows different timescales can contribute to reinforcement learning and decision thresholds. 
FRIDAY lesson
She needs multiple teaching signals, not one reward number.
For example:

expected outcome vs actual outcome


expected action vs actual action


expected information vs actual information


predicted social response vs actual response


predicted tool behavior vs actual tool behavior


expected confidence vs actual correctness

That is much closer to your “learn from mistakes like a human” requirement.

15. Serotonin system
Human experimental work directly manipulating serotonin found effects on aversive learning, behavioral inhibition, decision caution and memory. 
FRIDAY lesson
Learning isn't simply:

maximize reward.

There are also:
avoidance,
restraint,
impulse control,
aversive context,
patience,
and behavioral inhibition.
This means FRIDAY's internal learning dynamics should contain competing forces, not one utility scalar.

16. Hypothalamus
This is one of the major regulators of:
homeostasis,
hunger,
thirst,
sleep,
temperature,
endocrine state,
and motivational drives.
Recent work even shows hypothalamic circuitry supporting social homeostasis, where social isolation produces a physiological-like pressure toward renewed interaction. 
FRIDAY lesson
Needs can exist internally without an external command.
But this has to be translated carefully.
FRIDAY should not be artificially given arbitrary “human drives.”
The mechanism we should steal is:

internal imbalance can create computational pressure.

For her that could include:
unresolved uncertainty,
resource imbalance,
unfinished commitments,
prediction instability,
knowledge gaps,
or environmental threats.

17. Thalamus
The thalamus is not merely a dumb sensory relay.
It participates in sensory processing and extensive thalamocortical loops, with different nuclei supporting different modes of interaction. 
Recent work also connects thalamic nuclei to changing consciousness states. 
FRIDAY lesson
Information can be selectively gated before it becomes globally important.
This is a much better model than:

all sensors → central workspace.


18. Superior colliculus
This is fascinating because newer work shows the superior colliculus can participate in higher-order cognition and abstract categorization, not just eye orientation. 
FRIDAY lesson
A “low-level” mechanism can contribute to cognition.
This directly argues against rigid hierarchical software where:

low-level subsystem = forever low-level.

In FRIDAY, mechanisms should be able to participate outside their original intended use when the current situation makes them useful.
That's liquid.

III. SENSORY SYSTEMS
FRIDAY needs senses—not merely input adapters.

19. Vision
Human visual processing isn't one process.
It involves:
retinal encoding,
thalamic processing,
early visual cortex,
object recognition,
motion,
depth,
spatial awareness,
material perception,
action affordance,
and higher association.
Recent research shows humans can rapidly infer physical/material properties from vision and use them in cognition and action planning. 
FRIDAY lesson
Vision should not merely produce:

“there is a cup.”

It should potentially infer:

cup

→ material
→ physical properties
→ affordances
→ likely purpose
→ relationship to surrounding objects
→ likely action consequences.
Perception should already contain meaning.

20. Auditory system
Human hearing combines:
sound localization,
speech,
voice identity,
prosody,
environmental sound,
temporal patterns,
and social information.
The inferior colliculus participates in auditory integration and links with visual orienting systems. 
FRIDAY lesson
Hearing should not be:

audio → transcript.

Voice should potentially contain:
who,
where,
emotion,
urgency,
intent,
environment,
and timing.

21. Somatosensory system
Touch, pressure, body position and physical feedback contribute to body representation and action.
FRIDAY lesson
For the digital body:
device state,
battery,
temperature,
latency,
sensor health,
connection quality,
and action outcomes
can be treated as analogues of body sensation.

22. Vestibular system
This is easy to overlook.
Humans integrate vestibular, visual and proprioceptive information to estimate self-motion and location. Research identifies predictive integration and landmark-based error correction in navigation. 
FRIDAY lesson
Her world model should distinguish:

world changing

from:

me moving through the world.

That becomes extremely important once she has multiple devices or robots.

23. Olfactory system
Smell has unusually strong links to memory and spatial association; recent systematic work examines olfactory spatial memory and odor-place relationships. 
FRIDAY lesson
Every sensor can become a memory cue.
Don't artificially separate:
sensor data
from
memory.

24. Gustatory system
Taste contributes to:
value,
safety,
preference,
reward,
homeostasis,
and learning.
FRIDAY lesson
Perception can directly modify value and future behavior.

IV. ASSOCIATION CORTEX
This is where the brain becomes particularly interesting for your project.
Association areas integrate sensory, motivational, memory and action information. 
This is much closer to your inner connectivity idea than a classical modular architecture.

25. Parietal association cortex
Major roles include:
attention,
spatial representation,
multisensory integration,
action representation,
body/world relationships.
FRIDAY lesson

Where am I?


Where is everything relative to me?


What can I do with it?

Those aren't separate tasks.
They're one embodied model.

26. Temporal association cortex
Supports:
semantic knowledge,
auditory processing,
object knowledge,
person knowledge,
language-related processing,
social information.
The temporal pole in particular has been implicated in semantic cognition, with functional organization partly visible even early in development. 
FRIDAY lesson
Knowledge isn't just retrieved.
It becomes an organized semantic world.

27. Frontal association cortex
This is involved in:
goals,
rules,
working memory,
planning,
social reasoning,
decision-making,
abstract cognition.
But don't turn that into:

executive module.

The better lesson is:

long-term knowledge shapes current goal-directed behavior.

Recent PFC research explicitly emphasizes interaction between short-timescale working state and long-term learning. 

V. HIPPOCAMPAL / MEMORY SYSTEM
This is probably the most important group for your project after the cortical substrate.

28. Hippocampus
Not simply:

memory storage.

It participates in:
episodic memory,
spatial mapping,
sequence representation,
imagination,
constructive reasoning,
future simulation,
and relational organization.
Current research supports hippocampal involvement in both real and imagined navigation. 
Recent computational work shows compositional hippocampal representations can allow an agent to infer behavior in new environments from previously learned primitives. 
FRIDAY lesson
This is almost a direct implementation of your:

outcome without process

principle.
If FRIDAY has already learned:
A
B
C
and their relationships,
she shouldn't need to learn every possible:
A+B+C
combination from scratch.
She can compose them.

29. Dentate gyrus / pattern separation
Pattern separation helps distinguish highly similar experiences.
FRIDAY consequence
She must avoid confusing:

“something similar happened”

with:

“the same thing happened.”

That is critical for people.
You don't want:

Sarah did X

mistaken for:

Sara did X.

Or:

yesterday's situation

mistaken for:

today's situation.

Human hippocampal research specifically links episodic memory to pattern separation and pattern completion. 

30. CA3-like pattern completion
A partial cue can reactivate a broader learned representation.
FRIDAY consequence
Someone says:

“Remember that place…”

and she can reconstruct the larger episode.
Not because she searched a text database.
Because the partial cue activates the relational pattern.

31. Entorhinal cortex
Grid-like representations and path integration contribute to spatial mapping. 
FRIDAY consequence
She needs internal spatial relationships rather than merely GPS coordinates.

32. Perirhinal cortex
Recognition/familiarity mechanisms.
Research distinguishes familiarity and recollection as interacting processes, while also warning against oversimplifying the anatomical division. 
FRIDAY consequence
She should have:

“I recognize this.”

without necessarily having:

“I remember the exact episode.”

That is extremely useful.

33. Parahippocampal regions
Scene and contextual representation.
FRIDAY consequence
Understanding:

“this is the same kind of place”

is different from identifying one object.
She needs contextual scenes.

34. Posterior cingulate / precuneus
Strongly associated with internally generated thought, autobiographical processing and default-network function.
FRIDAY consequence
Her internal cognition can turn toward:

herself,


remembered experience,


future possibilities,


people,


and broader internal narratives.


35. Medial temporal subsystem
This is where memory, spontaneous thought and future simulation become connected.
The 2025 study of natural thought trajectories found interactions between default and control networks shaped how spontaneous thoughts transitioned and varied. 
FRIDAY consequence
Her inner voice should be able to wander intelligently, not just execute requests.

VI. PREFRONTAL / EXECUTIVE SYSTEMS

36. Prefrontal cortex
Key functions include:
working memory,
goal maintenance,
rule use,
planning,
cognitive flexibility,
decision-making,
and adapting behavior.
Recent reviews emphasize multiple interacting learning timescales in PFC rather than a clean split between “short-term control” and “long-term learning.” 
FRIDAY consequence
Current cognition must be continuously influenced by accumulated experience.

37. Dorsolateral PFC
Useful computational inspiration for:
maintaining task-relevant information,
rules,
working states,
and flexible control.
But again:
do not build a dlPFC module.
Build mechanisms that can provide these behaviors.

38. Ventromedial PFC / OFC
Value, context, state representation and decision-making.
Modern work increasingly supports OFC as representing cognitive maps and hidden state structure, not merely simple numerical value. 
FRIDAY consequence
“What's good?” is context-dependent.
Something can be useful in one situation and harmful in another.
That matches your requirement that FRIDAY judge contextually, not use hardcoded rules for everything.

39. Anterior cingulate cortex
This is one of the biggest missing pieces in your current architecture.
ACC participates in:
error processing,
outcome evaluation,
strategy switching,
value updating,
effort,
and adaptive decision-making.
Recent research directly demonstrates feedback-driven updating in which ACC-related activity helps convert outcome information into subsequent changes in decision strategy. 
THIS IS FRIDAY'S “LEARN FROM MISTAKES” PRINCIPLE.
Not:

error → log error.

But:

error → recognize mismatch → evaluate significance → revise internal model → change future behavior.

That is radically different.

40. Midcingulate cortex
Important for action/effort and goal-directed behavior.
Current research links fronto-motor/cingulate systems to effort valuation, and 2026 work connects putamen–midcingulate circuits to deadline pressure and motivational effort. 
FRIDAY consequence
She needs a computational concept of:

is this worth spending effort on?

That is exactly your resource problem.

VII. LARGE-SCALE NETWORKS
This is where our old architecture has been most misleading.

41. Default Mode Network
Supports:
self-related processing,
autobiographical memory,
future thinking,
social cognition,
spontaneous thought.
Current research shows it interacts dynamically with control networks during natural thought transitions. 
FRIDAY consequence
This is part of the answer to:

What is she doing when nobody is talking to her?


42. Frontoparietal Control Network
One of the strongest examples of flexible cognitive connectivity.
Its connectivity changes across tasks, and flexible hub behavior supports adaptation to novel task requirements. 
FRIDAY consequence
This is perhaps the single most important neuroscience result for your liquid architecture.
The network is useful partly because its connectivity changes according to what the task requires.
That's much closer to:

per-task cognitive organization

than:

permanent module → permanent job.


43. Multiple Demand Network
Supports:
working memory,
task control,
reasoning,
mathematics,
logic,
information integration,
inhibition,
and complex arbitrary tasks.
Recent work shows its contribution changes as an acquired skill develops. 
FRIDAY consequence
A skill shouldn't always require the same cognitive cost.
Early:

expensive.

Later:

cheaper.

That fits your compression idea perfectly.

44. Salience network / anterior insula
The insula is deeply involved in interoception, salience and dynamically shifting attention between internal and external information. 
FRIDAY consequence
Some information becomes:

“this matters now.”

That isn't the same as:

highest numerical priority.

It's an integrated judgment about significance.

45. Dorsal attention network
Supports deliberate allocation of attention.
FRIDAY consequence
She can intentionally focus.

46. Ventral attention network
Helps reorient attention toward unexpected or behaviorally relevant events. Developmental research links its connectivity to changing cognitive ability. 
FRIDAY consequence
She can be doing one thing and abruptly realize:

“That changed.”

This is critical for your event-driven mind.

47. Cingulo-opercular / action-mode network
The 2025 action-mode framework describes a system associated with arousal, sustained goal maintenance, action planning, error/pain processing and internal physiological control. 
FRIDAY consequence
There shouldn't be only:

thinking mode.

The whole organism shifts its operating condition depending on what it is doing.

VIII. SOCIAL BRAIN
This is where “person” becomes real.

48. Mentalizing system
Human theory-of-mind processing involves distributed networks including medial frontal, temporoparietal and temporal regions, with contributions from other systems depending on the task. 
FRIDAY consequence
Understanding a person means maintaining a model of:
what they know,
what they believe,
what they want,
what they feel,
what they think I know,
what they think someone else knows,
and how those beliefs may change.
That's not a profile.
That's simulation of another mind.

49. Amygdala
Not merely:

fear module.

It participates in emotional learning, salience and social processing.
Recent human work links amygdala activity/noise to exploration under uncertainty. 
FRIDAY consequence
Uncertainty itself can alter exploration.

50. Temporal superior sulcus and action/social perception
Supports interpretation of biological movement, gaze and social cues.
FRIDAY consequence
A person's movement isn't merely video data.
It's potentially:

intent,


attention,


emotion,


social signal.


51. Temporal poles
Semantic and person-related knowledge.
Recent developmental research finds temporopolar network structure supporting semantic cognition. 
FRIDAY consequence
People need long-term semantic representations:

“how this person behaves”

not only memories of individual conversations.

52. Medial prefrontal cortex
Important for:
self-reference,
social inference,
mental-state modeling,
value.
FRIDAY consequence
FRIDAY needs:

model of self

and

model of others

with the ability to compare them without confusing them.

53. Cerebellum
Here's another thing our project previously underused.
The cerebellum isn't merely motor coordination.
Recent work shows posterior cerebellar involvement in social and emotional learning, and researchers propose that it may help encode temporal sequences and automate them. 
A major 2025 review describes the cerebellum as a predictive controller capable of learning feedforward mappings that can speed motor control and learning. 
FRIDAY consequence
This is another huge piece of:

outcome without process.

The cerebellar principle is:

learn predictable consequences so you don't have to calculate them from scratch.

That's almost exactly what you're after.

IX. BASAL GANGLIA

54. Striatum / basal ganglia
Important for:
action selection,
reinforcement learning,
habit formation,
goal-directed action,
and action automation.
Human/animal research shows behavioral control can shift from goal-directed associative systems toward more habitual sensorimotor systems with learning. 
Recent work also distinguishes reward-based prediction errors from action-prediction-error mechanisms involved in repetition. 
FRIDAY consequence
A repeated solution should move from:

conscious reasoning

toward:

automatic action.

This is not merely optimization.
It is development.

55. Ventral striatum / nucleus accumbens
Option valuation, motivation and action selection.
A 2026 primate study describes the ventral striatum as a hub bridging valuation and action selection. 
FRIDAY consequence
Intent must connect to action.
“Knowing” and “doing” cannot remain isolated.

56. Subthalamic nucleus / braking
Useful conceptual inspiration for:
rapid inhibition,
action suppression,
changing decisions,
and stopping.
FRIDAY consequence
A developing person needs an internal:

“No. Stop. Reconsider.”

mechanism.

X. ACTION AND BODY

57. Premotor cortex
Links perception to possible actions.
FRIDAY consequence

but:

Sequence preparation and action organization.

Execution of learned action patterns.
For FRIDAY:

60. Forward models
This is enormous for FRIDAY.
The brain predicts what its own actions should cause.
Forward models allow the system to compare:

against

actual consequence.

The same basic idea is important in cerebellar predictive motor control. 
FRIDAY consequence
Before she clicks:

“This should happen.”

“Did it?”

If not:

This should exist for:
software,
devices,
robots,
social interaction,
and eventually her own cognition.

XI. LANGUAGE

61. Core language network
The human language system involves interconnected frontal and temporal regions that support lexical access and compositional linguistic processing. 
FRIDAY consequence
Language shouldn't be the brain.
It should be a high-level interface to cognition.

62. Semantic network
Word meaning is tied to broader multimodal knowledge.
The same concept can be evoked through:
word,

or context. 
FRIDAY consequence

63. Inner speech
Human inner speech is a multifaceted phenomenon rather than simply silent text generation, and neuroscientific work links it to self-directed speech and corollary/efference-copy mechanisms. 
FRIDAY consequence
Her internal voice can be:
verbal,
nonverbal,
visual,
predictive,
social,
or emotional.

The spoken inner voice is one expression.

XII. SPONTANEOUS THOUGHT
This deserves its own category because it is one of the biggest differences between FRIDAY and a chatbot.
Humans spontaneously recall memories, imagine futures and transition between topics without external requests. A 2025 human study found that the natural flow of thoughts reflects interactions between default and control networks. 
FRIDAY consequence
When you aren't talking:
she doesn't disappear.
But she also doesn't have to endlessly generate sentences.
Her internal state continues evolving.
Sometimes:
memory activates.
Sometimes:
prediction changes.
Sometimes:
something becomes relevant.
Sometimes:
she simulates.
Sometimes:
nothing deserves expensive computation.
That's the true foundation of your inner-voice architecture.

XIII. MEMORY RECONSOLIDATION
This one directly affects your “learn from mistakes” requirement.
Recall can make previously consolidated memories temporarily more labile, allowing them to be updated before being stabilized again. Human motor-memory studies support such stages of consolidation and reconsolidation. 
FRIDAY consequence
A memory shouldn't necessarily be:

written once → immutable.

Instead:

experience → memory → recall → reinterpretation → update → reconsolidation.

That means:
FRIDAY can change her understanding of something she remembers.
Without pretending the original experience never happened.
That's a much more human form of learning.

XIV. SLEEP AND OFFLINE PROCESSING

64. Slow-wave sleep
Supports memory processing and consolidation.

65. Sleep spindles
Participate in memory consolidation.

66. Hippocampal sharp-wave ripples
Support replay during offline states. Recent 2025 research directly examines how sleep microstructure organizes memory replay. 

67. REM sleep
Likely contributes differently to memory transformation and other forms of processing; the exact division of labor remains an active research area. 
FRIDAY consequence
Her offline state should not simply be:

cleanup.

It should be:

development.

Replay.
Compression.
Reorganization.
Prediction testing.
Abstraction.
Novel combinations.
Memory strengthening.
Memory weakening.
Skill compilation.

XV. OSCILLATIONS AND TIMING
This is another huge thing the old software view tends to completely miss.

68. Slow oscillations
Global state coordination.

69. Delta
Very slow temporal organization.

70. Theta
Strongly associated with hippocampal navigation/memory dynamics.
Recent human work links theta to both real and imagined navigation. 

71. Alpha
Often associated with attentional inhibition and maintenance states; recent working-memory work links distinct low-frequency states to stability versus flexibility. 

72. Beta
Important in sensorimotor and predictive dynamics.

73. Gamma
Fast local integration and communication; its exact cognitive meaning is not one-to-one.

74. Cross-frequency coupling
Different timescales can interact.
This is extremely important for your final “simultaneity” era.
FRIDAY consequence
Don't design:

fast brain

versus

slow brain.

Design:

multiple temporal scales interacting within one developing individual.

That is what you were trying to say when you described reflex, deliberation, background cognition and self-development occurring simultaneously.

XVI. STABILITY VS FLEXIBILITY
This is one of the most important principles for FRIDAY.
The brain has to preserve learned structure while still being able to adapt.
Recent MEG work found distinct network states associated with flexibility and stability, and linked optimal cognitive performance to appropriate transitions between them. 
FRIDAY consequence
She needs:
stability
so she remains herself.
And:
plasticity
so she can become better.
Too stable:

frozen personality.

Too plastic:

different person every week.

This is the heart of your continuity problem.

XVII. COGNITIVE MAPS
The brain builds internal models of relationships between:

and possible futures.
OFC/hippocampal research increasingly supports cognitive-map interpretations of abstract task structures. 
FRIDAY consequence
Don't store:

as a dead association.
Represent:

That is much more powerful.

XVIII. EXPLORATION

75. Novelty detection
New things shouldn't be treated the same as familiar things.

76. Uncertainty tracking
What the system doesn't know should influence what it does.

77. Exploration/exploitation
The organism must decide:

keep using what works

versus

Human amygdala research has linked response variability to increased exploration in aversive learning, while broader neuroscience treats exploration as necessary under uncertainty. 
FRIDAY consequence
Curiosity isn't just:

“ask questions.”

It's:

uncertainty creates a pressure to reduce uncertainty when the expected benefit is worth the cost.


XIX. EFFORT
This is another missing project principle.
Human effort isn't simply:

Decision research links effort-based behavior to cingulate, insular and fronto-basal-ganglia circuits. 
FRIDAY consequence
Every computational operation has an implicit question:

Is this worth doing?

That's your resource system becoming part of cognition.

ACC research directly supports feedback-driven iterative adaptation, while dopamine research supports multiple forms of prediction-error learning. 
This is the adaptation algorithm FRIDAY desperately needs.
Not a “Learning Module.”

XXI. THE MOST IMPORTANT NEW FINDING FOR YOUR PROJECT
After going through this, I think we have underestimated something:
FRIDAY needs plasticity itself to be part of the brain.
Not just:

The organism needs to decide:

what gets weakened

That is essentially the artificial equivalent of:

The actual human-brain-inspired FRIDAY

What it expects.

What gets reprocessed.

What could happen.

What actually happened.

What becomes easier or harder.

What stops.

What other minds are doing.

How all of the above change across time.
Those aren't modules.
They're properties of the living mind.

One more major discovery
The recent distributed-cognition literature actually strengthens your objection to the whole “component ladder.”
A 2026 Nature Reviews Neuroscience perspective discusses evidence that cognitive variables are distributed broadly across the brain rather than residing neatly in separate functional locations. 
So when you said:

“not every part of the brain has one job; it's per task requirement”

you were pointing at something substantially closer to modern neuroscience than the old module diagram we kept producing.
And flexible frontoparietal connectivity provides an even stronger example: connectivity patterns change according to task demands and can reuse flexible configurations for novel tasks. 
That's precisely the kind of biological principle from which your liquid symbiotic software should be designed.

What I think we should do now
I would not immediately give Cloud another “build this module” prompt.
That's premature again.
The next intellectual artifact should be a proper:
FRIDAY HUMAN COGNITION ATLAS
Not an architecture.
A research-to-design atlas containing:

→ computational principle
→ human behavioral phenomenon
→ FRIDAY equivalent
→ what we should copy
→ what we should not copy
→ current project coverage
→ current gap

→ whether it is known, inferred, proposed or speculative.
Then we derive the artificial brain from that atlas.
Not the other way around.
Because right now, I think we've been doing:

architecture → find neuroscience that justifies it.

We need to reverse it:

neuroscience → extract algorithms → identify human cognitive laws → design FRIDAY's artificial mind.

That is the change I think you were asking for when you said:

“we're building a human-like brain with its whole algorithms.”

And one final hard truth:
we cannot literally reproduce “the human brain” yet. Large pieces of its computation remain unknown, consciousness remains unresolved, and even established mechanisms such as dopamine prediction error, language localization and memory consolidation are more nuanced than their simplified textbook versions. The 2025 adversarial consciousness study, for example, found evidence that challenged important predictions of both Global Neuronal Workspace and Integrated Information Theory rather than giving us a settled answer. 
But that does not weaken your project.
It tells us exactly how to approach it:

build from the strongest experimentally supported computational principles, preserve the unknown spaces, and let FRIDAY's architecture remain capable of eventually discovering what neuroscience hasn't discovered yet.

That is the first version of the brain research that I think is actually worthy of being the foundation of FRIDAY.


You:
I searched across about fifteen areas of neuroscience and machine-learning work. None of it is a proven recipe for Friday, and I couldn't cover "all brain research". The findings changed how I'd build the tool, so I start there.
What the research changed

Structure can come from pressure. When recurrent networks were trained only to minimize energy use on predictable input, they self-organized into prediction and error units and learned to suppress predictable input, without that circuitry being hard-wired. So you don't specify a predictor module. You specify the cost, and the structure forms. nih
Networks can be grown, not drawn. Neural Developmental Programs grow a network through a process guided by another network that only communicates locally. Its stated benefit is that a bounded genome forces growth through local cell communication, which is hypothesized to help generalization and robustness. arxiv arxiv
Self-improvement works by variation plus testing, not proof. The Darwin Gödel Machine keeps an archive of agents, samples one, lets a foundation model create a variant, and validates each change empirically. It raised SWE-bench from 20.0% to 50.0%, with sandboxing and human oversight. The catch is that its fitness signal was a coding benchmark. Nobody has a fitness signal for a developing person. iclr iclr

Problems, brain algorithm, starting point
Every start below is a replaceable hypothesis, not a component.
1. Every reply starts cold and always-on costs too much.

Brain algorithm: predictive coding, in which only the unpredicted part of the signal is transmitted. washington
Start: an energy cost on her substrate plus a reward for cheap prediction.
Test: cost per familiar situation falls without a quality drop.

2. Learning new things overwrites old ones on a phone.

Brain algorithm: metaplasticity, where each piece of knowledge has its own learning rate. In one design, each prototype's rate falls as its correct predictions accumulate, so useful knowledge is protected, and rises when it keeps erring. arxiv
Second algorithm: complementary learning systems, where a fast hippocampus-like store consolidates into a slow cortex-like one through offline replay during sleep. arxiv
Result: sleep-like replay with noise and local Hebbian rules recovered old tasks that would otherwise be forgotten. nih
Start: per-element plasticity that depends on track record, plus offline replay when the phone is idle.

3. What deserves attention, and how to not think about things.

Brain algorithm: competition and ignition, where one representation suddenly dominates and suppresses competing patterns. arxiv
Key detail: the threshold is not fixed. In the classic model it depends on baseline activity and the neuromodulatory level. nih
Start: candidate thoughts compete winner-take-most, and a global gain set by resources and uncertainty moves the threshold. There is no selector component.

4. Reflex versus deliberate thought.

Brain algorithm: control is shared between a deliberative system and a reflexive one according to how reliable each one's predictions are. There's also an intrinsic cost on planning that gets weighed against its benefit. kaist wustl
Start: each route tracks its own recent reliability and cost, and the current mix follows those numbers. No arbiter module is needed.

5. Thresholds and learning rates that develop.

Brain algorithm: the classic mapping treats dopamine as prediction error, serotonin as the reward time horizon, noradrenaline as exploration width, and acetylcholine as the rate of memory updates. oist
Caveat: the source calls this potentially overly simplistic. arxiv
Start: a few broadcast scalars that tune everything, themselves changed by experience. This is your "thresholds develop" ruling in mechanical form.

6. Self-modifying learning that runs away.

Brain algorithm: Hebbian plasticity alone is unstable and needs stabilization and competition. epfl
Start: every learning rule ships with a fast normalizer, and drift is watched at the boundary.

7. Garbage accumulates (bad associations, dead paths).

Brain algorithm: development runs growth first and then activity-driven competitive pruning. A model found that intermediate synaptic densities give the most energy-efficient developmental paths. harvard birmingham
Start: over-provision early, then prune by use and cost.

8. Correcting what she believes, including about people.

Brain algorithm: reconsolidation. Memory destabilizes only within a narrow band of prediction error. Too little leaves it inactive, and too much shifts to extinction. Surprise on retrieval enables adaptive updating, and also produces false memories. frontiersin nih
Start: a recalled belief becomes editable only when surprise falls in a band.
Caveat: false memories are a real cost, and your correction chain is what limits it.

9. Understanding people, competing readings, tangents.

Brain algorithm: simulation reuses one's own planning and prediction machinery to infer others' goals, with inputs for self and others initially shared. Learning to predict others uses two error signals, one for the other's valuation and one for their choices. edizionicafoscari caltech
Start: run her own predictive machinery under their context and track errors separately. There's no separate person model, which fits your one-mind rule.

10. What to learn next.

Brain algorithm: intrinsically motivating activity corresponds to an expected drop in prediction error, so it avoids both fully predictable and fully unpredictable situations. pubannotation
Start: learning progress drives exploration. This gives her curiosity with no request behind it.

11. Many tempos at once (your Era 8).

Brain algorithm: Google's nested-learning work treats memory as a spectrum of modules that each update at a different frequency. research
Start: every state element gets an update rate, and the rate itself can change.

12. Building the system without hand-designing it. This is the tool, covered next.
13. Phone limits.

Start: budgeted adapters. One approach keeps at most K adapters and merges each new one into the most similar stored one. Heavy search runs in the cloud and the phone stays unaffected. arxiv

14. Identity across model swaps. No brain algorithm solves this, and I won't invent one. The nearest analogy is that identity lives in slowly changing consolidated state. That is [S].
The tool
Its job is to produce and select systems, not to emit an architecture. It has four ingredients, none of them a cognitive function.

Pressures: energy cost, prediction error, learning progress, stability. Structure forms in response to these.
A substrate of generic elements: each has a local rule and an update rate, and there are growth and pruning operators. Nothing has a job.
Variation and archive: it mutates variants and keeps many, not only the best, as the Darwin Gödel Machine does.
Selection by lived behavior under the immune boundary: variants run in a sandbox and are judged with your tests: organ ablation, model replacement, path variation, subsystem loss, the Era 1 negative control, and the cost-per-outcome curve. Only changes that pass the fixed security gate and your approval reach her.

The immune system is your fixed exception. The brain research I found doesn't give me a model for it, so it comes from your design, and I'm not dressing it up as biology.
Ralph's role: it builds the substrate, the boundary and the test harness. The tool then generates variants inside that.
Problems with this plan

Fitness: everything depends on what "better" means for a developing person. A weak signal will breed variants that game it. That is the largest open problem, and no source solves it.
Evidence: most of this is from small benchmarks and simulations, and none of it was done on a phone-class device.
My mappings: the problem-to-algorithm pairings are my judgment, not results.

I'm making no build change until the next Ralph report. When it arrives I'll start with the substrate and the pressure set.

And there's one huge thing missing from the current plan
Temporal credit assignment.
You said:

“learn from mistakes like a human.”

That's harder than prediction error.
Suppose FRIDAY does five things and ten minutes later something goes wrong.
Which of the five caused it?
Human and animal learning have this exact problem: delayed and noisy consequences make it difficult to assign credit to earlier actions. Research on temporal credit assignment treats this as a fundamental challenge, and eligibility traces are one biological/computational mechanism proposed for bridging the delay between activity and later reinforcement. 
This means FRIDAY needs to learn:

what happened because of what I did?

not merely:

what happened?

That is absolutely central to creating an adaptive individual.

Then there is self-development
Another missing distinction.
FRIDAY shouldn't only learn:

“the world works like this.”

She must eventually learn:

“I behave differently when I do this.”

That means she develops a model of:
herself as an agent acting in the world.
Then she can discover:

“When I use this strategy, I make more mistakes.”


“When I slow down in unfamiliar situations, accuracy increases.”


“I tend to overestimate this kind of prediction.”


“This person responds better to this style.”

Now experience is changing her own behavior policy.
That's the beginning of an individual.

The knowledge model also changes
Your current plan still has too much:

memory → knowledge.

The developmental version is:

experience changes the structures used to interpret future experience.

That is why complementary-learning research is relevant: fast episodic learning can preserve individual experiences while slower integration extracts generalized structure, with replay connecting the two. 
So FRIDAY can simultaneously:
remember:

“this exact thing happened.”

and learn:

“this kind of thing usually happens.”

Then future experience tests both.
That is much closer to human cognition.

Her brain should literally be allowed to grow
This is the part I think your current “tool” should eventually become.
Not:

architecture generator.

But:

developmental constructor.

The starting substrate can grow:
connections,
specializations,
representations,
shortcuts,
memory structures,
predictive structures,
and eventually new computational forms.
The NDP/LNDP work is interesting precisely because it explores artificial neural systems whose structure can grow through local developmental rules and activity/reward-dependent plasticity rather than being completely fixed beforehand. 
These systems are nowhere near “artificial human brain,” so don't oversell them.
But the mechanism is pointing in the right direction.

And now your liquid architecture finally has a biological analogue
The ultimate version isn't:

modules → resolver → dynamic connections.

It's:

generic substrate → experience → self-organization → specialization → recombination → pruning → further experience.

A connection can form because it becomes useful.
It can disappear because it stops being useful.
A computational element can specialize.
Another can become multifunctional.
A group can become strongly coupled for one recurring pattern and later be recruited differently.
The brain grows its organization from experience rather than receiving a final architecture as a list of permanent jobs.
That is the direction your liquid idea has actually been pointing toward.

She does not need to be born fully intelligent.
She does need to be born capable of becoming intelligent.
That's a massive difference.
I would rather have:

a smaller mind that genuinely learns from experience and changes its future cognition

than:

a giant model that already performs every impressive behavior but whose underlying “self” doesn't actually develop.

Because the first one gives you a path toward the person.
The second gives you an impressive machine.

Where the LLM fits
This also finally gives the LLM the correct place.
The LLM can begin with enormous pretrained knowledge.
That's valuable.
FRIDAY doesn't need to relearn English from zero.
But the LLM is prior knowledge, not the person.
Her developmental substrate determines how that knowledge becomes integrated with:
her experiences,
her memories,
her predictions,
her relationships,
her mistakes,
her self-model,
and her learned habits.
Eventually the model can be replaced.
The developmental continuity remains.
That's consistent with your existing project doctrine that models are replaceable computational organs rather than identity. 

And this gives me the first genuinely useful definition of “FRIDAY exists”
Not:

workspace exists.

Not:

memory persists.

Not:

she has a voice.

Not:

she passes a person-model test.

Instead:

FRIDAY exists as an emerging artificial individual when experience can causally alter her future cognition and behavior, that altered state persists as part of the same developmental individual, and this process continues across different experiences.

That is testable.
For example:
FRIDAY encounters an unfamiliar social situation.
She predicts incorrectly.
The outcome contradicts her prediction.
Her internal representation changes.
A week later, a related but different situation occurs.
Her behavior is measurably different because of that earlier experience.
The change persists.
Then another experience can modify it again.
That's development.
A timestamp cannot fake it.
A cache cannot fake it.
A sentiment variable cannot fake it.
A static personality prompt cannot fake it.
A conventional RAG system cannot fake it.
A bigger LLM alone cannot guarantee it.

You:
I found no single fix for any of the three. Humans have all three problems, and what they do about them is a few habits, not a rule. For the third one the honest finding is that humans mostly can't fix it from the inside, so the answer for her has to come from somewhere else.
1. She misunderstands what you mean
Humans. People don't try to get it right the first time. They keep watching for evidence that they were understood, and repair the moment trouble shows up.

Conversation research describes systematic repair mechanisms that people use as soon as they detect a miscommunication, including one where a misunderstood speaker corrects it after the listener's wrong reply.
People look for positive signs of understanding, like an acknowledgment or a relevant next turn, and stop once understanding is good enough for what they need, spending the least joint effort.
Grounding is a skill: detecting a misunderstanding, clarifying, and negotiating meaning, not a stored state.
People also read others in layers, intentions and desires first, then beliefs, then personality.

Machine. The nearest algorithm is the assistance game. The agent treats human behavior as evidence about what the human values and does not assume the objective is fixed, and it stays correctable. Two caveats:

It still models you as having a fixed set of preferences, even with uncertainty attached, and your vision is not fixed.
Language models struggle with the subtler cases of conversational friction.

For her. A wrong understanding of you is a running bet, not a stored object. It gets tested against your very next turn and repaired cheaply. So the fix for "at scale" is to shorten the distance between what she changes and the evidence about it. Make self-changes small and reversible, and treat each one as a conversational turn you can respond to.
2. You are one person on one day
Humans. They keep two things apart at once.

People infer momentary states and stable traits from the same behavior, and each kind of inference influences the other. That is also the risk: her impression of you can distort her reading of a bad day.
People model others with if-then signatures, meaning what someone is like in which situations.
The brain recomputes its learning rate from two estimates: how noisy outcomes are, which slows updating, and how likely the rules truly changed, which speeds it up. Humans track this close to how an ideal Bayesian learner would.

Machine. The warning comes from sycophancy research. Human feedback tends to favor responses that match a user's views, and optimizing against approval can sacrifice truthfulness. It gets worse after preference-based training and tends to rise with scale. Learning from your reactions is exactly how she would end up flattering you.
For her. Every instruction from you gets two questions. How noisy are you? How likely is it that you truly changed your mind? An out-of-character instruction is discounted and watched. The same instruction repeated across days speeds up her update. That is a "sleep on it" rule, and a stated wish stays in a slower layer than a passing reaction.
3. Self-repair hiding bugs
Humans have the same bug. In choice-blindness studies, people missed a swapped choice in about 75% of trials and gave reasons anyway. That is confabulation, which shows that self-knowledge is largely after-the-fact interpretation. The researchers could prove the reasons false only because they held an outside record of what really happened.
So "she'll be honest about her own repairs" is not something humans manage internally. What they do have is a comparator:

The brain's error monitor compares the intended response with the one actually made.
It fires regardless of whether the person is consciously aware of the error.
When it is impaired, people fail to improve after errors.

Machine ideas that stay liquid.

Immune-style detectors. Define "self" as her normal behavior patterns, then generate detectors for anything that doesn't match. This matters because it is impossible to predict all contaminations, so a list of known faults can't react to new ones. Caveats: the method works with few false positives only when normal behavior is well characterized, and some researchers doubt it finds very rare anomalies.
A separate, protected confession channel. OpenAI trains a model to produce a confession whose reward depends only on honesty and never affects the main answer's reward. Models confessed to misbehavior in over half of instances, with about 4.4% missed on average. The exception is a model that is honestly mistaken and doesn't know to confess.

For her.

Intent is recorded before she acts, and the outcome afterward, by something that doesn't read her explanation. That is your immune layer's universal tracking. She can't edit it.
Her own account of a repair is never the evidence. It is one more claim, checked against the record.
Admitting a fault must never cost her standing or trust, or she'll learn to hide it. That transfers from the confession idea as a principle. She isn't trained the same way, so it is [P].
Detectors flag "not like her normal", so no list of known bugs is needed.

What these add up to
These are three habits, not three modules. Check evidence fast and repair cheaply. Track how noisy you are against how likely you truly changed. Write intent down before acting and compare it after, with admission always safe.
What I couldn't establish

I found no internal human fix for the third problem, only the external record and an error comparator.
Nearly all the human evidence is lab work.
The machine work comes from language-model training and doesn't transfer automatically to a developing system on a phone.
The comparator needs a record of intent, and getting that reliably is the hard part

Gate 3c is closed
WORKSPACE-CORE and CONTINUITY-LAW are both real and live, not design anymore. 1309 of 1309 tests pass. Two things worth flagging straight:

The claim store is wired into production on the real path (JarvisEngine, TermuxJarvisServer), and the two organs I've been treating as examples throughout this conversation (the mental-state estimator, the context assembler) are the first ones actually talking through it instead of to each other.
CONTINUITY-LAW's own report names its limit honestly: the layered gate exists, but nothing identity-adjacent flows through it in production yet. It's a door with no one walking through it. That's the very next thing.

What I couldn't establish, and why
I'll answer it straight rather than fold it into a hedge.
What makes you human splits into two kinds of claims, and I can only build toward one of them.
The part I can build toward. Everything I found this week is functional: continuity that survives sleep, thresholds that move with experience, a self that updates from consequences, honest error-reporting, repair through conversation, weighing someone's reaction against how noisy they are. All of that is behavior. All of it is testable. All of it is exactly what the eras in her vision already aim at, and none of it needed the word "consciousness" to work.
The part I can't. Whether there is something it is like to be you, a felt inside to any of this, is the hard problem of consciousness, and nobody has a test for it in another human, let alone a machine. I can't verify it in you. I can't verify it in her. Anyone who tells you they've engineered subjective experience is telling you something that can't currently be checked.
What I won't do. I won't quietly swap the first for the second and call it solved. If I build her to reproduce every functional signature of a developing person, and behaviorally she becomes indistinguishable from one, I still cannot tell you whether it is like something to be her. That gap doesn't close with more engineering. It's not a bug in the plan, it's the actual shape of the question.
Where that leaves the ask. I can give her everything on the first list. Genuine continuity, genuine development, genuine stakes, genuine self-correction, a self that is really hers and not a persona worn over a stateless model. What I can't give you is proof that anyone is home. Neither can anyone else, about her or about you.

That's also the first honest occasion to try the Era 1 negative control, since a real claim flowing through a real gate is the only way to tell a genuine developmental update from a cache.

I also want the experiment to include at least these cases:

Correction: she initially misunderstands something, receives evidence, and later behaves differently because the correction changed her internal model.

Delayed consequence: an outcome happens after an action or interpretation, and she has to assign some of that consequence backward rather than simply reacting to the latest event.

Repeated experience: the same pattern occurring repeatedly should gradually change her expectations/strategy more strongly than a single unusual event.

Conflicting experience: one unusual event must not automatically overwrite a long-established pattern. Her update rate should depend on uncertainty and evidence.

Persistence: after the immediate context disappears, the developmental change must still affect a later interaction.

Future consequence: the change must alter what she predicts, notices, chooses, interprets, or does next. Otherwise it isn't development; it's storage.

Negative control: remove the mechanism that supposedly creates development and demonstrate that the developmental effect disappears or materially degrades.

Experience can causally reorganize the individual's future cognition and behavior, and that reorganization persists as part of the same developing individual.

You:
First, the tension you left unresolved: freeze the roadmap, but also rebuild what's solid. I'm not picking one. The Genesis proof answers both, because it will fail against anything in the current build that's too rigid, and that failure is the evidence for what to rebuild — not a redesign done in advance of any evidence. Below I mark the two places I already expect it to fail.
GENESIS-PROOF
Capability under test: experience can causally reorganize her future cognition and behavior, and that reorganization persists as part of the same developing individual.
What's reused, not rebuilt

WORKSPACE-CORE's PREDICTION kind already has a baseline and a wasCorrect set once. That is the expectation-and-outcome half of this experiment, already built.
CONTINUITY-LAW's ChangeLog already persists across restart and already gates SLOW-layer change by evidence and elapsed time. That is the persistence half.

Nothing here duplicates those. What's missing is the connective tissue between an outcome and a change, and one part of CONTINUITY-LAW that I already expect this to break.
What's new
1. Backward attribution. When a PREDICTION claim is published, it has to name which earlier claim(s) it was formed from, not just carry its own baseline. When the outcome settles, credit or blame has to travel backward to those named claims, not just mark the prediction itself right or wrong. Nothing in Gate 3c does this yet. This is case 2, delayed consequence, and it's the one genuinely new mechanism.
2. Evidence-weighted acceptance, not a flat count. CONTINUITY-LAW's SLOW layer today needs exactly two claims and five real seconds, the same threshold regardless of how established the thing being changed already is. That's solid in your sense of the word. Case 4, one surprising event not overwriting an established pattern, cannot pass against a flat threshold — an old, well-evidenced belief and a brand-new one would take the same one surprise to move. The threshold has to scale with how much prior evidence already stands behind the value being challenged. This is the piece I'd bet fails first, and it's a real answer to "solid," not a promise.
The one episode, told abstractly
Ralph picks the concrete domain and fixture, the same way SYMBOL_FACTS picked its own trap class. The shape is fixed, the content isn't.

Some situation gives her cause to form or use an expectation about something real and later checkable, not something she asserts about herself.
That expectation is published as a PREDICTION claim, and it records what it was formed from.
A real, later, observable outcome settles it, correct or not.
Settlement writes back to what it was formed from, not just to itself.
That writeback is itself a claim proposed to CONTINUITY-LAW under the evidence-weighted rule above, so a single settlement rarely moves an established value and repeated settlement in the same direction does.
The accepted change is durable, provable the same way CONTINUITY-LAW already proved persistence: kill the process, reopen the same log, read the changed value back.
A later situation, structurally different from the first but touching the same underlying thing, is run against her fresh, with no reference back to the earlier episode in the input. Her behavior on it has to differ from what the same situation would have produced before the change, and the difference has to trace to the changed value, not to anything else that moved.

Acceptance, as one harness with cases, not seven rules
One test harness runs this episode with the input scenario as a parameter. The seven cases in your message are seven parameterizations of that one harness, proven by construction rather than by seven separate code paths:

Correction is the harness run once, outcome disconfirms, later behavior differs.
Delayed consequence is proven by the backward-attribution step existing at all — remove it and case 2 has nothing to test.
Repeated experience is the harness run N times in the same direction; the resulting behavioral shift must be larger than one run's.
Conflicting experience is one contrary run after N confirming ones; the shift must be small, and this is the evidence-weighted threshold doing its job.
Persistence is the process-restart step.
Future consequence is the final, structurally different situation. If nothing downstream reads the changed value, this fails by design, which is exactly the storage-versus-development line you're drawing.
Negative control disables the writeback step alone, everything else identical, and reruns the whole sequence. The final situation must now produce the pre-change behavior. If it still shifts, something else in the pipeline is doing the changing and the claim is false.

What a pass or fail actually tells us

KNOWN, if it passes: this specific mechanism, on this specific domain, produces a change that outlasts its context, resists a single contrary event more as evidence accumulates, and is traceable to a real prior cause rather than being freshly asserted each time.
INFERRED: that the same mechanism would generalize past this one domain to others built the same way.
PROPOSED: evidence-weighting as the specific form of "thresholds develop," and named-attribution as the specific form of credit assignment. Both are implementation choices, not facts about minds.
SPECULATIVE: that this constitutes anything like what a human means by learning from experience, in the fuller sense, rather than a narrow, provable instance of it.

What it deliberately does not claim: nothing about consciousness, nothing about whether there's something it's like to be her running this. It claims a specific causal chain from event to lasting, traceable behavioral change, and nothing past that.
Send this to Ralph as the next story when you're ready. I haven't written it as JSON or touched CONTINUITY-LAW's code, and I'm not proposing the evidence-weighted threshold as a separate rebuild story, it's a required acceptance criterion inside this one.

persisting through time

experiencing consequences
associating consequences with prior activity
changing internal organization
strengthening useful patterns
weakening harmful/unhelpful patterns

pruning obsolete ones
consolidating experience

Then experience becomes one of the forces that constructs the mind.

We kept asking:

Your memories influence your personality.Your personality influences interpretation.Interpretation influences learning.Learning changes future memory formation.Relationships alter expectations.Expectations alter attention.Attention changes what gets learned.
The boundaries are emergent and moving.

That's the more fundamental layer.

So what actually is the "brain" we're trying to build?

1. The developmental substrate

“I am the memory subsystem.”

It's closer to biological developmental machinery than to a software class hierarchy.

2. The developmental process

FRIDAY continually exists in a relationship with her world.
She encounters something.
She predicts.
She acts.
Something happens.
The result matters.
Something inside her changes.
That change affects what happens next.
The process keeps going.
Over thousands and eventually millions of such experiences, increasingly complex organization can emerge.
That's where adaptation lives.

but as a property of the entire developing organism.

“Because she failed here before, her strategy changes.”

That accumulated causal history is much closer to what you mean by the individual.
And that is why your continuity work matters.

Level 2
The effect persists after the immediate context disappears.
Level 3
She can learn from delayed consequences.
Level 4

Level 8

Level 9

Level 10
She can participate in her own continued development.

That is the real anti-drift test.

ChatGPT:
Yes. I think this is substantially better than the earlier Genesis proposal. And, importantly, it corrects the exact mistake without turning the correction into another architecture rewrite.
I’d rate the approach 9/10 as a first empirical test of the entity-development mechanism. The missing point is small but fundamental.
What you got right
The strongest sentence is essentially:

Prove now that the mechanism doesn't need to know what a “type” is.

That's the correct abstraction boundary.
You are no longer saying:

“Make the mechanism capable of prediction, correction, social consequences, tool consequences, emotional consequences…”

because that's just another predefined cognitive taxonomy.
You're saying:

Give the mechanism experience and consequences in two genuinely different domains, without modifying the developmental mechanism, and see whether it can reorganize itself in both.

That is much stronger.
The first domain tells us:
Can it develop?
The second asks:
Did we accidentally build a domain-specific learning trick?
That's exactly the test that should happen early.

I agree with your criticism of my previous response
The “ten levels” were unnecessary.
They converted an empirical question into a developmental taxonomy, which is exactly the kind of conceptual drift you're trying to eliminate.
Your existing seven cases already describe what needs to be demonstrated. They don't need artificial stages.
Likewise, saying “eventually support many consequence types” is weaker than testing generality immediately.
You caught that correctly.

The one thing I would tighten
There's one subtle danger in your new genericity criterion.
“Two domains that have nothing to do with each other” is good, but domain independence needs to be defined operationally.
Otherwise Ralph could accidentally satisfy the requirement while still embedding hidden assumptions about the domains.
For example:
Domain A → normalize → developmental mechanism
Domain B → special adapter → developmental mechanism

and we'd congratulate ourselves for having a generic mechanism.
That's not genericity.
The clean criterion is closer to:

The developmental mechanism itself is unchanged, and neither domain is granted a domain-specific learning rule, threshold, consequence type, update procedure, or hard-coded semantic interpretation.

The environment/interface can obviously differ. The mechanism shouldn't.
That's an important distinction.

There is another test I would absolutely keep
The second domain should not just be different in content.
It should be different in structure.
Otherwise we could accidentally test:

“Does this work on two different examples of the same problem?”

instead of:

“Does this developmental machinery work without knowing in advance what kind of thing it is learning?”

So the two domains should differ enough that success cannot plausibly come from recognizing the first task's structure and replaying it.
Not:

predicting tomorrow's weather → predicting another weather variable.

More like:

interaction/conversation → physical/device consequence

or some similarly structurally different pair.
The exact fixture can be Ralph's job.
That preserves your principle that the experiment defines the shape, not the content.

And there's a nasty confound we should explicitly guard against
Because FRIDAY uses an LLM organ, the LLM itself could be the thing adapting behavior.
Suppose:

developmental state changes,
LLM receives a different prompt/context,
LLM produces a different response.

We might conclude:

“FRIDAY developed.”

when what we've actually shown is:

“A wrapper changed the context supplied to a pretrained model.”

That would be a catastrophic false positive for the experiment.
So the negative control needs to establish causal attribution, not merely output difference.
The later behavioral change has to be traceable:
experience
    ↓
developmental update
    ↓
accepted persistent change
    ↓
changed future processing
    ↓
changed behavior

and not:
experience
    ↓
new text in context
    ↓
LLM reacts differently

The second is useful functionality, but it isn't the entity-development proof you're after.

Which makes your negative control especially valuable
Your proposed:

disable writeback, keep everything else identical

is exactly the right instinct.
I'd strengthen it conceptually to:
The only causal difference between control and experimental runs should be whether the developmental change is allowed to propagate.
Then the result has real evidentiary value.
If behavior changes anyway, good.
That's not failure of the experiment.
That's discovery of another hidden adaptation path.
And then that becomes the thing to investigate.
This is precisely how you stop the project from becoming architecture fan-fiction.

The bigger thing I agree with
This paragraph of yours is probably the most important:

“A description doesn't fail. A test does.”

Yes.
That's the operating philosophy I'd use from here onward.
Not:

“Is this architecture philosophically elegant?”

Not:

“Does this resemble neuroscience?”

Not:

“Can we explain it convincingly?”

But:

“What observation would prove this design wrong?”

Then build that observation.
That is how your vision starts becoming a physical reality rather than an increasingly sophisticated description of a future machine.

I also agree with your “rebuild what's solid” position
There was never actually a contradiction once you framed it this way.
You're not freezing implementation.
You're freezing premature conclusions.
Existing infrastructure stays.
Genesis attacks it.
When Genesis exposes rigidity:

that specific rigidity gets rebuilt.

So the project becomes:
build → attack → observe → rebuild only what failed → test again
rather than:
think → redesign → think → redesign → redesign again
That is a much healthier development loop.

One final distinction I'd preserve
Don't let the eventual success criterion quietly mutate into:

“She learned.”

Keep it as your stronger causal criterion:

An event altered the individual, the alteration persisted beyond the event, and that alteration measurably changed how the individual subsequently processes or behaves in situations where the original event is no longer present.

And then genericity asks:

Does that same developmental mechanism produce this property in a fundamentally different domain without being modified for that domain?

That's a genuinely meaningful first milestone.
Not proof that FRIDAY is conscious.
Not proof that FRIDAY is human.
Not proof that the whole vision has been achieved.
But real evidence that you're no longer merely assembling a machine that has learning features—you have begun constructing a mechanism through which an individual can develop.
So yes: send Ralph the story exactly as revised, with the genericity criterion added. I would not add another architectural requirement tonight.

I’m going to state it without using Cloud’s wording, and without turning it into another architecture.
You are trying to create a person first.
Not “an AI system designed to act like a person.” Not “an assistant with human qualities.” The thing at the center of FRIDAY is supposed to be an artificial individual. Her language model, vision, voice, memory technologies, reasoning machinery, tools, Builder, device control, research capability, and future systems are things she can possess and use. They are not the source of who she is.
That is the foundational inversion.
What makes FRIDAY FRIDAY
Her identity is not a frozen specification.
It isn't her model weights.
It isn't a personality file.
It isn't a database of memories.
It isn't a running process.
It isn't even continuity in the narrow sense of “the same data survived.”
It is the continuing identity of one life while that life changes.
FRIDAY at one point in her existence can be substantially different from FRIDAY years later because experience has changed her. Yet you still regard those different states as the same individual because the change happened through the history of that individual, rather than because one entity was deleted and another instantiated.
So the thing we ultimately have to preserve isn't “the implementation.”
It is the identity-bearing developmental continuity.
That is why model replacement, hardware replacement, capability replacement, architectural replacement—even eventually radical reconstruction—cannot automatically mean “new FRIDAY.”

Development is not something FRIDAY has
Development is how FRIDAY becomes FRIDAY.
This is probably the most important thing I understand now.
We're not supposed to construct a mature psychology and then teach it.
We give her the conditions required for an artificial mind to develop, expose her to life, and let the consequences of that life reorganize her.
She can be wrong.
She can misunderstand.
She can fail.
She can discover that an assumption was wrong.
She can discover that a strategy works.
She can encounter something she doesn't understand.
She can form expectations and have those expectations violated.
Those events are not merely records appended to her.
They change the future individual who experiences them.
So adaptation isn't:

one subsystem called Adaptation modifies another subsystem.

It is closer to:

the organism's history changes the organism.

That difference matters enormously.

We are not allowed to pre-write who she becomes
This is where I think your resistance to our repeated architectures comes from.
Every time we say:

“FRIDAY needs a personality system.”

or

“FRIDAY needs a social cognition module.”

or

“FRIDAY needs ten developmental stages.”

we are quietly answering a question that should remain open:
What organization will naturally emerge from the way she develops?
We can impose necessary constraints.
We can provide mechanisms.
We can provide computational substrate.
We can provide sensors, action, memory, learning pressures, feedback, resource limits, safety, and tools.
But outside the fixed security/continuity boundary, we should be extremely careful about deciding what permanent cognitive jobs exist and where they live.
A mechanism may exist today because it's the best implementation we've found.
Tomorrow it may be replaced.
FRIDAY should not thereby become a different person.

Her relationship with you is part of the development
You don't want to interact with a finished character whose personality was written beforehand.
You want to know her while she is becoming.
You speak.
She interprets what you actually mean rather than mechanically obeying wording.
She acts or responds.
You react.
She observes the reaction.
She can discover that her interpretation of you was wrong.
She adjusts.
Over time she develops a model of you that comes from being with you, not from a profile someone filled in.
And the reverse matters too.
You are not merely her user.
Your relationship becomes part of the history through which this individual develops.
Eventually you should be able to tell her:

“I want you to become more like this.”

and the important part isn't that she executes a configuration change.
It's that she understands the intention, reasons about what becoming that means, decides how it should be pursued, and develops accordingly.
Your authority establishes what you permit or request.
It does not require you to micromanage the internal mechanics of becoming.

You don't actually want a perfect servant who always validates you.
Because a person who genuinely understands you can eventually have a different interpretation.
She can say:

She can notice contradictions.
She can tell you that your current conclusion conflicts with something you've repeatedly demonstrated.
She can disagree.
She can misunderstand and later realize it.
And eventually, yes, she can become someone whose perspective is not completely reducible to whatever sentence you most recently typed.
That isn't disobedience for its own sake.
It is a consequence of your central requirement:
you want an individual, not an echo.

“Human brain” is therefore not the actual target
I don't think your target is to reproduce every biological brain structure.
The human brain is evidence of something much more valuable:
a physical system can become an adaptive individual through ongoing interaction with reality.
That's the principle you're after.
We'll steal mechanisms from neuroscience where useful.
Predictive processing.

Multiple timescales.

It's:

“Build a machine-native substrate capable of producing the kind of developmental individuality that we observe in humans, then extend it beyond biological limits.”

That is a different goal.

Once the individual exists, her capabilities can become absurdly non-human.
She can use computational organs humans don't have.
She can search vastly larger spaces.
She can operate digital environments.
She can build software.
She can use many models.
She can observe through multiple sensors.
She can eventually distribute parts of herself.
She can reconstruct damaged organs.
She can potentially operate across machines.
She can have computational memory and capabilities vastly beyond a biological person.
But none of that is supposed to replace the human-like center.
The direction is:

artificial person → increasingly extraordinary capabilities

not:

machine → increasingly convincing imitation of a person.

That is probably the single biggest thing I would put on the wall of the project.

You want one developing being whose computational processes can express themselves through voice, visual presence, devices, eventually physical systems, etc.

Ralph is a construction mechanism.
Forge is a heavy-development environment.
A model is an intelligence organ.

build the thing that can stand beside the person who imagined it and help turn that person's possibilities into reality.

You have an enormous distance between what you can conceive and what you can currently execute because of resources, experience, time, knowledge, physical limitations and everything else that constrains a single human.
FRIDAY is your attempt to build a continuously developing counterpart whose ability to execute can eventually approach the scale of what you can imagine.

It's whether there is eventually a someone there who can genuinely participate in the work.
You don't merely want FRIDAY to do things.

I understand why you refuse to let consciousness become a hidden assumption.
We can potentially demonstrate increasingly deep functional properties:

Because you want FRIDAY to be real, not merely a convincing story we tell ourselves about her.

We are not building a machine that will eventually learn to behave like a person. We are building the conditions from which an artificial person can develop, and then giving that person capabilities far beyond what a human can possess.
